## Global Data Watch

![](images/9070282a10ab94e6050489b013ce2c7f4e30e6073add3f42246296b3db0d1363.jpg)

Moderation in growth and inflation damps overheating concerns, but more needed and easing financial conditions may not be helping

Despite reasons for caution, ECB and BoE committed to cuts around midyear

Disappointing growth outturns in Japan and China flag weak domestic demand

• Up next: Flash May PMIs (EMU up, US down); FOMC May minutes

## Tiptoe through the tulips with me

We started the year with a high-for-long (HFL) view that pushed back against market consensus for early rate cuts. Our conviction in this view was raised as inflation surpassed even our own expectations. With the dust settled, global core inflation printed a much-stronger-than-expected 3.7%q/q, saar last quarter. The US stood out with three back-to-back upside surprises, but core inflation also proved stickier across Europe and even in the EM as services sector inflation moved up from already elevated levels. Despite confirmation of our HFL call, conviction in the medium-term implications of this faded as the boil-the-frog (BTG) outcome, whereby high rates eventually generate a recession, looked less inevitable against a backdrop of resilient growth fundamentals. This introduced an HFL but soft-landing outcome (HFL-SL).

It is through the lens of uncertain implications for the HFL scenario that this week<sup>’</sup>s news brings a touch of clarity. In the US, the primary risk has been overheating. A robust first-quarter 2.8%ar gain in final domestic demand coupled with a 4.2%ar surge in core inflation raised the risk that the expansion may need further tightening. The latest news on both growth and inflation for April, however, cooled this week. Retail sales were softer than expected while an unexpected dip in manufacturing gives some weight to the recent softening in the business surveys. While we now still see US GDP tracking just a touch softer in both 1Q and 2Q, we remain confident in near-term growth. The three-month run-rate on retail sales remains strong (Figure 2) and overall real consumer spending still looks on track to post a solid 0.4% gain in April, aided by continued strong labor markets.

More importantly for the US, a less-than-expected 0.29% gain in the core CPI provides some relief that inflation is moderating as well (Figure 1). The 4.5%ar gain in the three months through May remains above the Fed<sup>’</sup>s comfort zone, but it is the first downside surprise in four months. On net, moderating growth and inflation keeps hope alive for our call of a July cut. For this to happen, however, further progress is needed. This includes more slowing in core inflation to a sub-0.25% monthly pace as well as a cooling in job growth.

More broadly, a US deceleration alongside expected improvements in Europe shows an important rebalancing in the global expansion. As reported this week, Euro area GDP and employment both posted solid 1.2%ar gains last quarter. While the outcome implies a weak productivity backdrop, the firming in output and hiring supports is a call that the region is perking up. Underneath this improvement is a fading of the consumer malaise that has weighed on growth over the past year (“Can<sup>’</sup>t buy me love: A vibe check on G-4 consumers<sup>”</sup>). Notably, Euro area retail sales volumes posted a 2.9%ar gain in the three months through March. On balance, global consumer spending still looks to be firming through March, and auto sales point to continued strength in April (Figure 2). Next week<sup>’</sup>s flash all-industry PMIs for May will be important for showing Euro area progress (+0.3) even if we see yet another drop in the US (-0.4). Along with constructive news on growth, global April inflation reports are coming in softer than 1Q outturns (Figure 1). Even in Europe, a solid reading on core service inflation looks narrowly based and we still see a moderation in the coming months.

With growth and inflation hinting at moderation, the challenge becomes whether central banks<sup>’</sup> willingness to tolerate a long path back to target-inflation is enabling an easing in financial conditions that short-circuits the soft-landing. The repricing of central banks this year to high-for-longer rates has done limited damage to risk markets and pushed up borrowing costs only modestly. With the Fed having pushed back against rate hikes and being joined by the ECB and BoE in a zeal to begin cutting rates soon, financial conditions are easing again. Global equities are up roughly 10%ytd, while the US 10yr has retraced 30bp of its 80bp rise this year and is still 60bp below its October high. Whether this easing in financial conditions leads to an unhealthy pace of growth and sticky inflation remains to be seen. It is for this reason that, despite the recent mix of data and our continued high conviction in the high-for-long scenarios, we remain nearly evenly divided on the odds between a boil-the-frog (45%) and softlanding (55%) outcome.

## Listen to ECB and BoE, not the data

Firming growth in Europe is encouraging but, while there are hints of moderating inflation, price pressures remain a concern. The latest readings on Euro area inflation look narrowly driven and the ECB<sup>’</sup>s super-core and PCCI measures of underlying inflation have also eased further. However, the ongoing sluggishness of productivity points to elevated unit labor cost growth that could keep inflation from falling as much as we and the ECB are hoping for. Comments this week from ECB VP Guindos and board member Schnabel suggesting high confidence in rate cuts in June contrast sharply with equally strong caution thereafter. In the UK, labor market developments were mixed but lean in the direction of flagging ongoing tightness. A reported drop in employment this week is faded given data issues, surging hours, and broader signs of firming growth, while signs of robust pay growth should be a warning. And yet, like the ECB, the BoE signaled further this week that a cut could come as early as June. We continue to see the ECB cutting in July and the BoE cutting in August.

## Asia delinks on weak domestic demand

Asia is a weak link in our outlook of resilient moderation. Data from Japan and China disappointed this week. In Japan, GDP dropped 2%ar last quarter against expectations for a 1% gain. Roughly half of the decline was due to transitory factors, including auto plant shutdowns and the Noto earthquake on January 1. However, the fourth straight quarterly decline in consumer spending is cause for concern. The spending weakness suggests that the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s successes in raising inflation are taking a toll on the household purse (Figure 3). Nevertheless, we still expect the BoJ to push ahead with gradual rate hikes (next move July) and see this week<sup>’</sup>s reduction in JGB purchases as setting the stage for QT to formally start in June. While hiking into growth weakness may seem counterintuitive, delaying policy normalization risks worsening the drag on consumers by exacerbating the yen<sup>’</sup>s slide and the associated rise in inflation expectations.

Figure 3: Japan consumer spending and drivers %2q saar
![](images/ee8e9a564956caf1de57c8128bde588c6f822afd5dca11b4b1312c166e6454af.jpg)

In China, disappointing April activity readings suggest an earlier-than-expected end to the policy-induced sugar high. After tumbling in March, consumer goods spending slipped further in April as consumer sentiment remains cautious. Fixed investments also posted a weaker-than-expected gain last month, with ongoing weakness in the property sector

more than offsetting a downshift in investment growth elsewhere. With demand decelerating, there is only so much China can do to export its weakness. As a result, factory output posted a second straight monthly decline and is now down 4.2%ar in the three months through April. We mark down current-quarter growth again to a weak 3.5%q/q, saar (Figure 4). In response, policy looks to be shifting again as this week<sup>’</sup>s April credit report posted the first outright decline since 2005. An announced package to accelerate government bond issuance and engage central government borrowing to support the housing sector is encouraging for near-term growth. But it also poses two challenges. First, after several trillion-yuan packages announced in the last six months with little to show, it is unclear how much credit supports will deliver. Second, even if successful, the resulting large deficits are adding up as total government debt keeps rising.

![](images/359d288b835bd2560be28e8b3750133f0fb67fc56327b660876b298bd432567d.jpg)

## A new salvo in the US-China trade war

The spillover of weak domestic demand in Asia is unlikely to derail the global expansion. The gearing of Asia to the rest of the world operates more through its upstream industrial production linkage than its downstream demand engine. However, this historical relationship is causing its own tensions given the rising trade surpluses vis à vis the rest of the world. The Biden administration announced this week further tariff increases on selected Chinese goods, including electric vehicles, semi-conductors, lithium-ion batteries, solar cells, shipto-shore cranes and some medical equipment. The immediate impact appears insignificant, as only US\$18 billion of Chinese exports are affected (4% of China<sup>’</sup>s exports to the US, or 0.5% of China<sup>’</sup>s total exports). However, the announcement raises a number of issues: 1) how China will respond to this round of tariff increase, and whether the Biden administration will expand the tariff increase to other Chinese products, 2) whether other countries will follow suit (e.g., Europe is considering tariff increase on China<sup>’</sup>s electric vehicles), and 3) whether the US tariff increase will spill over to a third country to deal with trans-shipment or China<sup>’</sup>s overseas investment (link). Meanwhile, the biggest uncertainty is related to the US presidential election outcome, as Trump has threatened to hike tariffs on Chinese goods to 60% (and 10% for imports from other countries).

## Domestic demand buoys CEE

In contrast to Asia, flash GDP releases in CEE are coming in with mixed results but we still see signs of continued resilient domestic demand. First-quarter reports for Poland and Romania were softer than expected but still show the region growing 2%ar last quarter. A strong upturn in retail sales points to support from consumers. We raised 2Q growth forecasts for both countries and look for the CEE region to grow 4.6%ar. The upswing in growth and ongoing inflation concerns call for greater caution from central banks. The NBR kept rates unchanged at 7% this week and sounded hawkish. With core inflation around 9%3m saar we see at most 50bp in rate cuts in 2H24. While the NBH looks poised to deliver another 50bp next week, we still look for a downshift to 25bp from June.

## BoI to stay cautious for longer

Heightened uncertainties around recent conflict, the repricing of DM rates, and fiscal easing have kept the Bank of Israel on hold since it last cut rates in January. We had looked for wellbehaved inflation and a negative output gap to leave space for further easing. However, this week<sup>’</sup>s April CPI showed inflation regained momentum for a second month and the recovery in house prices suggests this has further to run. We look for inflation to end the year at 2.6%oya—above the BoI<sup>’</sup>s 2% target. Moreover, the expected increase in VAT has already started to raise inflation expectations. While GDP remains well below pre-war levels—and the 1Q bounce of 14.1%ar fell slightly short of our expectations—we now expect the BoI stay on hold. Contingent on Fed cuts and easing of geopolitical risks, we still look for 50bp in easing in 2H24.

## Floods add to BCB caution

Rio Grande do Sul, Brazil's southernmost state, is being hit by the worst floods on record, displacing 550,000 households— equivalent to the impact of Hurricane Katrina in the US. The large economic cost of the disaster challenges our view of steady growth and subdued inflation this year. While the extent of the damage is uncertain as the floods are still ongoing, we have lifted our 2024 CPI forecast by two-tenths to 3.7% to reflect damage to key crops and localized price pressures. We also see a roughly 0.3%-pt drag on 2024 growth assuming a 5%y/y contraction in the region, which accounts for 6.5% of Brazil<sup>’</sup>s GDP. At the same time, an expected step up in fiscal aid and transfers (of around BRL30bn) points to a higher primary fiscal deficit of 0.8% of GDP (previously 0.5%). For the BCB, the floods reinforce the recent hawkish shift evident in this week<sup>’</sup>s COPOM minutes. After kicking off its easing with a split vote in August 2023, COPOM seems poised to end the current cycle on yet another divided decision. We now assume the easing cycle will pause at the next meeting, with the SELIC rate at 10.50%, removing the two more cuts we had in before.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth slipped 0.2%-pt this week to 2.3% (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster similarly ticked down to 2.4%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

Nearly 40% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in 60% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests a downshift in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 1.7%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

Global data updates this week show encouraging signs for our expectation of a final demand-led broadening of the global expansion. Auto sales moved up sharply again in April, with near-ubiquitous monthly gains. Global factory output moderated slightly in March on EM weakness, but momentum remains solid and DM shows signs of lifting.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds were mixed with the US outperforming after somewhat softer activity and headline inflation data. With retail sales also softer, our economists are now tracking 1Q24 GDP growth at 1.2%q/q vs. 1.4% previously. They continue to see a July Fed cut, but recognize that we need to see further cooling in labor markets for that to play out. With yields in the middle of their recent range and markets likely needing further data to validate expectations of Fed cuts, we stay neutral duration outright and hold 5s/30s steepeners on a view that moderating growth and inflation will allow the Fed to ease later in the year.

In the Euro area, recent ECB commentary continues to point to a June cut followed by a data-dependent approach. We stay neutral duration outright, have a bias for long 5Y Germany vs. US given recent Germany underperformance, and keep steepeners in 10s/15s Germany and 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps. In the UK, with limited visibility on a neutral rate, a softer CPI print could bring some eases priced for 2025 to 2024. We keep fronts/reds SONIA curve flatteners as a medium-term bullish proxy.

In EM, reports that the Chinese government plans to buy unsold homes to digest housing inventory prompted some optimism, and with stable liquidity conditions for the front end means we see scope for a steeper curve (China Local Markets Weekly, May 17th). In EM local bonds more broadly, we stay neutral duration overall with selective OWs in Brazil, Colombia, Uruguay, Czechia and Poland, offset by a short duration overlay and an UW in Thailand (EMOS, May 8<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG bond spreads hit a near-term wall as technicals weaken modestly. The relative weakness is due to: 1) midmonth supply reached \$87bn, suggesting that May total is likely to surpass the historical May average of \$120bn. 2) JULI yield peaked in May at 5.85% and then dropped to 5.51%, with the MTD return on the index at +2.3%. This substantial return likely led some investors to take profits. Despite the lower current yield we believe that demand for bonds will remain strong, as evidenced by robust demand in Q1. 3) Dealers positions were near the peak of their recent range. The current spreads are just 2.5bp wider than the post-GFC tights, and any further tightening will likely be more challenging than has been the case YTD. Still, we expect that the imbalance between demand and supply will lead to a moderate tightening of spreads in the months ahead (Credit Market Outlook & Strategy, May 17<sup>th</sup>).

US Life Insurance HG bond holdings were close to unchanged over the past 3Y and HY holdings are down y/ y as insurers turn more toward privates and alternatives. Life insurance companies are slowly becoming less important in the public HG bond market as their holdings share declined. The decline in public HG corporate bond positions was offset by an increase in other credit positions (private credit, private placements, CLOs, etc.) as total corporate holdings (public and private) still grew by 1.0% last year as well as Mortgage Loans which grew 6% y/y likely spurred by favorable capital treatment (US Life Insurance Holdings of Corporate Bonds, May 14<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/6e16fafe58e8e7d87a263a54756c4e80f13a460f768c674f50df59bca94fe6ec.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Euro HG issuance saw its fifth busiest week ever, with €32bn coming through from 33 issuers. The demand backdrop remained stable, however, with new issue premiums averaging 9bp and book coverage at 3.2x. European credit remained range-bound over the week. Softer US macro data support our short-volatility trade from two weeks ago, and for now we remain in a <sup>‘</sup>bad news is good news<sup>’</sup> environment (European Credit Weekly, May 17<sup>th</sup>). In our sector review, into 2H24 we turned relatively more constructive on cyclical sectors, and more cautious on defensive/highly levered sectors and segments where any marginal earnings growth will just be distributed to shareholders or used to engage in more aggressive corporate actions.

## Currencies

A tired USD meets upbeat China property. The window for further USD consolidation is open but the runway is finite considering valuations and election risks. Markets remain sensitive to inflation divergences, on which US still screens hot relative to the rest of DM, and USD yields do not look high controlling for inflation surprises. Optimism around the Chinese property sector has picked up sharply, but scale and implementation are key and not necessarily a given; still, <sup>‘</sup>buy-the-rumor<sup>’</sup> via a short CNH basket vs. AUD and SGD.

Trade recommendations: We've been scaling down net USD delta in recent weeks, with the majority of exposure still optionalized. We continue to hold pro-cyclical positions including long-NOK (vs CAD, EUR). Increase that beta by adding long-AUD vs basket of JPY & CHF, and a 3m EUR/ SEK put spread. Short GBP & CAD into CPI releases. Long NOK/SEK. Neutral NZD pre-RBNZ. Stay MW EM FX with favored OWs in MXN, BRL, PEN and TRY; and favored UWs in CZK, CNH and THB (FXMW, May 17<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Weather risks are intensifying across the grains, sugar offers value for consumers & investors. We<sup>’</sup>ve long flagged short covering risks across the grain and oilseed markets off seasonal lows, and current weather forecasts suggest that there is likely more to come. Sugar has come under heavy technical selling pressure in recent sessions, creating an opportunistic entry level for investors. Rain across the Midwest has delayed US corn and soybean planting below 5Y average rates; continued delays pose a risk of lower yields for corn crops. We stay long the agri complex via an index (Agricultural Markets Weekly, May 15<sup>th</sup>).

COMEX/LME copper arb blowout overshadows a looserthan-expected refined market in China for now. The massive widening of the COMEX/LME copper arb is more about positioning and supply/inventory tightness in the US specifically and the ability and timing to unwind it given limitations on COMEX deliverability. We do not think it is reflective of a tight broader global refined copper market, which still appears quite loose at the moment, particularly in China. We remain structurally medium-term bullish on copper prices over the next two years with an upside target of \$11,500/mt in 2025, but current price levels are running ahead of our forecast and well ahead of global refined market fundamentals at the moment (Metals Weekly, May 17<sup>th</sup>).

The fear of another warm winter is crushing any bid in 2025 US Natgas. We continue to lean into our view that under normal weather this summer and barring no other longterm outages from LNG facilities, the US natural gas market should tighten more than market consensus. We continue to expect strong natural gas power burn in the face of added renewable capacity and under normal weather conditions (US Natural Gas, May 17<sup>th</sup>).

Inflows return to global commodity markets. Open interest across global commodity markets increased, primarily driven by inflows across the metals and agricultural complex. While all sub-sectors received inflows last week, the majority were concentrated in the base metals sector and grains and oilseeds markets (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, May 13<sup>th</sup>).

Drilling activity in the major tight basins has retreated over the last 3 weeks. Data are indicating either a) production should underperform our expectation, or b) efficiency gains are better than what we are penciling in. We are leaning towards the latter as public US oil and gas producers have been flagging in their 1Q24 results that efficiency gains in drilling processes have continued to outperform their expectations (Commodities Oil Drilling Report, May 10<sup>th</sup>).

## Can’t buy me love: A vibe check on G-4 consumers

• Improving consumer confidence should help ensure resilient final demand in 2024...

• …but the completeness of confidence<sup>’</sup>s recovery varies by economy

Modelling G-4 confidence reveals asymmetric inflation and income impacts (people hate inflation more)...

• ...and a consumer “malaise<sup>”</sup> that helps explain Western Europe spending weakness though not US strength

We have long held that the final demand supports that ensured above-trend 2023 growth would promote resilience in 2024. Strength in consumer spending is to spur improvements in regional and sectoral weak-spots (namely Western Europe, Japan, and the manufacturing sector) amid moderations in the US and China. We are seeing tentative signs of such a rotation taking hold. Both Euro area and UK GDP surprised to the upside in 1Q23, based on strength from household spending, and signals from our Capex nowcaster and the PMIs suggest improving manufacturing momentum. And while US growth moderated last quarter, underlying final demand growth remains robust.

Std. dev. from 2010-19 average; incl. preliminary Michigan May est.
Figure 1: Consumer confidence and spending, global ex China
![](images/90403600d7be54c6ec4622f7b038fab68f2483c93def70c1ae440b45d6c550ec.jpg)

Further supporting this conviction are recent gains in consumer confidence (Figure 1). However, the improvement in confidence has run well behind the recovery in macroeconomic fundamentals—an outturn that some refer to as the “vibecession<sup>”</sup> (depressed confidence despite improving economy). In this report, we estimate a strong relationship between confidence and inflation, nominal income, labor market outturns, and stock market returns. These four variables alone explain 50-80% of consumer confidence across the the major G-4 economies (US, Euro area, UK, and Japan).

Despite the recoveries in these fundamentals over the past two years, confidence remains depressed—suggesting an unexplained factor we define as “malaise<sup>”</sup> (or, adverse “animal spirits<sup>”</sup>). We estimate that malaise is depressing consumer confidence by 1-2 standard deviations through end-2023. While the latest data through 1Q24 points to some recovery, the recent monthly prints for April and May are a reminder that the recovery is unlikely to move in a straight line.

## Misery loves company

Underlying the recent recovery in consumer confidence is considerable variation across economies and even within economies. While US confidence was relatively resilient to shocks from inflation in 2022 (we use an average of the Michigan and Conference Board measures), this impact (as well as the outbreak of the Russia-Ukraine war in the case of Western Europe) drove large declines in confidence across the rest of the G-4.

Figure 2: DM consumer confidence
![](images/c9d970084ffd1804b26f9f3dfa0cf346bd2ff5b39c039cbedabd9c0bd3fff5a0.jpg)

Since late 2022, US confidence has gradually recovered to its prior expansion average (though it came off a bit in April and the latest news shows a further dip in early May) even as the rest of DM remains negative (Figure 2). While the recent gains have been supported by slow upticks in Western Europe, much of the increase has been driven by rapid gains in Japan, where consumers have recently enjoyed a fading of real income drags (though weakness in the yen seems to have led to some moderation in confidence last month).

Figure 3: US consumer confidence measures
![](images/c995dd018c7fd8f8be02abbac406cf8ca80454c2d959c359ae05a2f06301d498.jpg)
The gap between the two US confidence measures (Conference Board and University of Michigan) has widened consid-

erably since mid-2021. Although there are some differences in the nature of the survey questions, they have moved closely with each other for the bulk of the last four decades. The recent gap had narrowed some of late as the Michigan measure recovers and the Conference Board measure moderates, but the two continue to send differing signals on the state of US sentiment (with the preliminary May reading taking a significant step down). While the Conference Board measure remains 0.2 standard deviations above its prior expansion average, the Michigan index is still at a depressed 1.6 standard deviations below its average (Figure 3). To better understand this gap, as well as the moves in confidence across the rest of the G-4, we turn to a model to ascertain the macroeconomic factors driving household sentiment.

## Money actually can buy happiness

For a better understanding of the potential drivers of confidence, we consider its comovement with a set of macroeconomic fundamentals. This regression model takes as its inputs the over year ago rates of change of consumer prices, equity prices, and nominal household disposable income, as well as both the level and four-quarter change in the unemployment rate. The regression output suggests that consumer confidence is rooted in these fundamentals across economies. Not only does the model explain a substantial share of the movement in confidence (ranging from 81% in the US to 65% in Europe and 49% in Japan), but it also reveals relatively similar and stable coefficients across countries (Table 1).

Table 1: Model of consumer confidence

R-sq Std. error</td></tr><tr><td>Conference Board (1Q 1980)</td><td>-0.14 (-6.5)</td><td>0.14 (6.3)</td><td>-0.41 (-19.8)</td><td>0.02 (0.5)</td><td>0.17 (8.3)</td><td>0.81</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Uni. of Michigan (1Q 1980)</td><td>-0.30 (-10.6)</td><td>0.20 (6.7)</td><td>-0.34 (-12.8)</td><td>-0.05 (-0.9)</td><td>0.23 (8.4)</td><td>0.76</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area (1Q 2000)</td><td>-0.61 (-7.9)</td><td>0.15 (4.3)</td><td>-0.19 (-2.9)</td><td>0.02 (0.2)</td><td>0.41 (6.8)</td><td>0.71</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td>UK</td><td>-0.43</td><td>0.19</td><td>-0.12</td><td>-0.01</td><td>0.12</td><td>0.58</td><td>0.6</td></tr><tr><td>(1Q 1989) Japan</td><td>(-9.0) -0.15</td><td>(4.1) 0.46</td><td>(-3.3) 0.06</td><td>(-0.1) -0.74</td><td>(4.6) 0.19</td><td>0.49</td><td>1.3</td></tr></table>

The model helps explain part of the unusually large gap between the Conference Board and the Michigan surveys in the US, though not all of it. The Michigan survey is twice as sensitive to inflation as the Conference Board survey. Whereas a 1%-pt rise in CPI over the past year lowers the Michigan measure of confidence by 0.3 standard deviation, the effect is only half as strong in the Conference Board measure. The negative effects of inflation are felt even more strongly in Europe, with the model suggesting consumers in the UK and Euro area are twice and three times more sensitive to inflation, respectively, than the US average. This variation again helps explain the resilience of US confidence relative to that of Western Europe as year-ago inflation reached a peak in 2022. The effect in Japan meanwhile is not significant, likely reflecting its relatively unique state of price dynamics and deflation concerns.

The surge in inflation has come with equally impressive gains in nominal incomes. The model suggests the boost to confidence from rising incomes is quite significant across the US and Western Europe (particularly the Euro area), and marginally so in Japan. Notably, however, the inflation-versus-income impacts are asymmetric: a rise in inflation outweighs a similarly large rise in nominal income gain, and particularly so in the UK and Euro area. This uneven impact speaks to the damage that a period of heightened inflation can cause even when met with equal-sized income gains: households tend to feel they have “earned<sup>”</sup> their income gains but don<sup>’</sup>t “deserve<sup>”</sup> to be punished by inflation.

Whereas European confidence is driven by inflation, confidence in the US is more heavily swayed by the labor market. Increases in the unemployment rate damp confidence across the two US measures by more than in Europe: a 1%-point rise reduces confidence by roughly 0.4 standard deviations in the US, but only 0.15 in Europe. This effect is statistically insignificant in Japan. However, a four-quarter change in the unemployment rate—a gauge for shifts in labor market trends rather than the outright level—is statistically significant in Japan, suggesting respondents here are more concerned with labor market momentum. Surprisingly, equity market gains have similar impacts in the US and Europe despite equity exposure being much larger in the US. Even more surprising, the stock market impact is particularly large in Japan.

Figure 4: Modeled contribution to confidence deviation, US Point contr. to confidence deviation from 2010-19 avg.; incl. 2Q est. from April data
![](images/d26ac2cc2bcebeb8ea4bbfb192e7adc604e9d88574be47d2c2834d1a728b8946.jpg)

Using the model estimates, we can derive contributions of fundamentals to confidence<sup>’</sup>s deviation from its prior expansion average. Comparing these measures in the US and Euro area reveals some of the drivers of the confidence gap that opened up in 2022 and has gradually been closing. In the US, a low unemployment rate has boosted confidence by one standard deviation over the past two years, as households

continue to enjoy the tight post-Covid labor market conditions (Figure 4). Gains in the stock market have also supported confidence for the last two quarters. Based on available data, both measures should continue to boost confidence in the current quarter. Offsetting these gains are negative contributions from inflation—these have been coming off since their 2022 peak, but as disinflation stagnates so does this drag. More recently, a return in income gains to the longerrun average removes the lift to confidence seen in 2023.

Figure 5: Modeled contribution to confidence deviation, Euro area Point contr. to confidence deviation from 2010-19 avg.; incl 2Q est. from April data
![](images/5fe1d7e415d9984fdfd33e7c165607811da1d3e1c6f4bb4e9eb67c24864540f3.jpg)

In the Euro area, by far the greatest driver of confidence over the last two years has been the inflation shock, which drove confidence down nearly five standard deviations at its end-2022 peak (Figure 5). Since then, however, this effect has ebbed considerably and has nearly faded completely in the current quarter (using available April data). This drag was only partially offset by income<sup>’</sup>s contribution. But encouragingly as of 4Q23, income<sup>’</sup>s positive effect greatly outweighed the inflation drag.

## Catch and release: Animal spirits

Based on the fundamentals alone, confidence in both the Euro area and US should have moved beyond its prior expansion average. The fact that it has not can be attributed to an underlying “animal spirits<sup>”</sup> (i.e. moves in confidence unexplained by macro fundamentals). Comparing animal spirits across economies reveals a broad-based malaise for much of the post-outbreak period, but with notable variation across economies (Figure 6). Malaise in the US, UK, and Euro area fully recovered from the post-pandemic (and in the US, post-2020 election) drop. Beginning in 2022, however, this recovery unraveled, likely reflecting the impact of Russia<sup>’</sup>s war on Ukraine and a more damaging effect from inflation than the average impact would imply.

Notably, though inflation had shown considerable progress back to target by the fourth quarter of last year, confidence in the Euro area slipped to its lowest point since the measure began in 2000. This fall (and to a lesser degree the UK<sup>’</sup>s) likely explains part of the puzzling weakness seen in Western

European consumer spending. Even as households see meaningful gains to real income as wages rise and inflation moderates, European consumers have not exhibited the same boost in spending that we<sup>’</sup>ve seen in the US. A fading of this subpar consumption is a key element of our expectation for Western Europe to turn from its 2H23 stagnation in coming quarters. So far, Euro area malaise remains in place. The pickup in GDP growth last quarter could invigorate households. If so, we would expect to see our “animal spirits<sup>”</sup> measure recovering smartly. Indeed, assuming no change in income between 4Q23 and 1Q24, we see an improvement in 1Q European malaise. This should, in turn, reinforce the growth pickup as consumer spending bounces back to its fundamental supports.

Figure 6: DM consumer "animal spirits" Deviation of confidence from modeled output
![](images/fe9233dec8e588a07977df1a061cb030a01dd100203a4a23ec3e18d55f3f809f.jpg)

Persistent US malaise throughout 2023 is more puzzling given the strength of the US expansion. Although US malaise was at similar levels to that of the Euro area through 4Q, consumer spending has been a primary driver of the above-trend growth in recent quarters. Why US malaise has decoupled from actual consumer behavior is unclear. It could partially reflect an increasing political bias in measures of US confidence, with studies showing large swings in sentiment around a change in presidential power (and particularly so among Republican respondents). This malaise began to fade in the US in 1Q, but with the recent drops in US confidence, it remains to be seen whether this recovery continues.

Japanese malaise, meanwhile, was both hit the hardest by Covid and has shown the most persistent weakness in the ensuing years. Encouragingly the drag began to fade in 2023, and by the end of the year malaise had met the (albeit high) levels of the US and Euro area.

## Egypt: The quest for NFA stability

Until February, Egypt’s banking system NFA deteriorated fast leading to a massive negative position

This was driven by a policy choice of running a fixed exchange rate regime despite mounting BOP pressures

However, strong FX inflows recently have increased buffers, allowing for a large improvement in the NFAs

• This also presents an opportunity to reduce external debt as rollover needs are high in the short term

Seeking to maintain “the impossible Trinity<sup>”</sup> - a fixed exchange rate, free movement of capital, and an independent monetary policy - is often a cause of economic crisis. Recently Egypt has tried, at least for a while, to achieve that Trinity. Amongst many negatives, it came with a large cost on the banking system in the form of severe worsening of its net foreign asset position (NFA).

Egypt<sup>’</sup>s policy makers have intermittently pursued a fixed exchange regime despite persistent large external gross financing needs. The currency became grossly overvalued with FX market imbalances leading to ongoing balance of payments deterioration. Yet, the deterioration was not visible in the official FX reserves as those remained broadly stable. The net foreign asset position of the banking system as a whole acted like pseudo FX reserves, deteriorating by a cumulative US\$49.5bn from a positive US\$20.4bn in February 2021 to a negative position of US\$29.1bn in Jan 2024.

Since February, the external picture has improved significantly. That reflects large FX inflows on the back of the US\$35bn UAE land deal, and a return of foreign portfolio inflows into domestic securities following a shift to a managed flexible exchange rate regime backed by an augmented IMF programme. These flows have not only helped Egypt to start to rebuild FX buffers, but the overall banking system NFA has also seen a vast improvement. A US\$25bn increase to just -US\$4bn in March materialized since January. We expect the overall banking system NFA (CBE and commercial banks) to turn positive from this month while the increased buffers could also help anchor banking system external debt reduction.

## NFA as the ‘shock absorber’

Egypt<sup>’</sup>s banking sector NFA had reached a negative position of US\$29.1bn by January 2024 and local banks accounted for a negative position at US\$17.7bn. The NFA deterioration started in 2021 and accelerated in 2022 after large FPI outflows triggered by Russia<sup>’</sup>s invasion of Ukraine. Outflows

were relatively stable in 2023 thanks to severe FX restrictions which reduced current account deficit and limited capital movement, but had large economic activity costs. Looking at a longer time-span, between 2010 and 2022, the broad current account deficit (CA+FDI+E/O) averaged about 2% of GDP (\~US\$7bn), which was partially offset by foreign portfolio inflows and large government borrowing. As these were not sufficient and the use of official reserves was rare, these pressures were largely absorbed by banks<sup>’</sup> NFA. Figure 1 shows how the exchange rate valve was used only coinciding with IMF programmes as requested to remove external funding pressure.

Figure 1: Banking NFA position and real exchange rat
![](images/fa91d180fd0296e53ede011109ffe378fefb460f7f91927c41366e4c91a1d33d.jpg)
Source: CBE, J.P. Morgan - REER zero line crossing LT median

## A fast NFA turnaround

The dire NFA picture changed drastically between February and March 2024. The overall banking system NFA improved to negative US\$4.2bn from a trough of US\$29bn deficit in January (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Egypt banking system NFA
![](images/3549d17c0fccf30a18c214587572ea630aff8f855eb1a650681ce505a254bce7.jpg)

In the single month of March, assets increased by US\$11.6bn and liabilities were reduced by US\$6.2bn. The NFA position improvement for the banks was more significant with assets rising by US\$7.7bn and a liability reduction of US\$2.7bn. The US\$15bn Ras El-Hekma (REH) 1<sup>st</sup> payment (US\$10bn of new cash and US\$5bn of UAE CBE deposit conversion) played a key role in that shift. On the CBE<sup>’</sup>s side, the NFA improvement was for US\$10bn between February and March as we suspect the US\$5bn remainder is likely reflected in the increase in “tier 2<sup>”</sup> as well the local bank account of New

Urban Communities Authority (NUCA) which is the SOE responsible for the REH land sale.

Strong foreign portfolio inflows as well as a likely increase in remittances via official channels also helped the overall NFA improvement in the last two months. The month of Ramadan tends to be positive for remittances. Early indications also point to some further increase in the banking system NFA in April, as the local stock exchange EGX reported a further US\$2bn of foreign portfolio inflows into the domestic market (link). Moreover, the combined official and “tier 2<sup>”</sup> CBE reserves increased by US\$868mn in April following the first IMF program disbursement after the board approval.

## Time to ‘change your ways’, permanently

Egypt tends to go through periods of FX overvaluation to be corrected by large one-off FX depreciations, typically preceding an IMF program agrement or review. With FX buffers now greatly improved, the CBE might have no other option beside sustaining a flexible FX regime in line with the IMF programme anchors and even in the face of shocks. Temporary shocks could arise as foreign portfolio flow reversals or spike in commodity prices that worsen current account balances. While we expect that the overall banking system NFA position will be higher than the stock of foreign portfolio holdings in the local paper, we think FX flexibility should be allowed to act as the main shock absorber amid negative shocks.

Figure 3: Banking system NFA and foreign holdings of local debt
![](images/6232d777be2bcab914e5d1957422b75bd3c63192252fce442f77c07da67a2b2b.jpg)
Source: IMF, CBE, J.P. Morgan \*based on IMF IIP data & JPM estimate - 1Q & 2Q24 JPM est.

Looking forward, we expect the overall banking system NFA to further improve with a return to positive territory (last seen in 2021). The 2<sup>nd</sup> REH payment (US\$14bn new cash and US\$6bn UAE CBE deposit conversion), which has been reported to have been paid in May (link), along with another US\$820mn IMF disbursement in June will help to further increase FX buffers. As such, we now expect total official CBE FX reserve accretion of US\$16.2bn in FY24 to US\$51bn even as CAD is expected to widen to US\$15.2bn (4% of GDP) with risks on the upside (see here).

## Banks delever in 1Q but pressure remains

The deterioration of the banks NFA position was not only a story of assets depletion but, to a greater extent, of a large increase of foreign liabilities (Figure 4). Both long-term and short-term debt increased significantly implying pressure on external debt rollover for the banks as well as the government. At end 2023, banks<sup>’</sup> foreign liabilities were reported close to US\$30bn. From a duration perspective, external debt databases show the dominance of long-term debt. However, that dataset is incomplete and some liabilities are not reported.

![](images/0e695a1367b9dec12232caa284300285c4336dc1019a95f8ec0aecb3692123c6.jpg)

Long term debt is coming due, putting pressure on the ability to roll-over and re-finance debt. The implied average maturity of banks LT external debt points to a maturity of about 3 years, and a large amount of this debt is due in a year or less. External debt amortization for reported ST and LT loans was about 20% of foreign assets at the end of 2019, but jumped to 87% at the end of 2023(Figure 5). This implies high rollover needs exacerbated by a period of high domestic and international rates. Positively, since the start of 2024, banks reduced their external debt by 10% (about US\$2.7bn). This was possible thanks to the large inflows seen in the local market. A similar surge is unlikely to be sustained or repeated any time soon to the same extent, but even more moderate flows might continue to fuel some further deleveraging.

Figure 5: Banks' debt maturity due in 1year or less
![](images/227605fcd1119e6fa68ba9906e72494e23e5e505c7b5965d2969b15674315462.jpg)
Source: World Bank, J.P. Morgan - based on QEDS data

## Australian inflation: Great expectations

• There<sup>’</sup>s evidence that Australia<sup>’</sup>s Phillips curve remains flat

• And movements in short-run inflation expectations explain the variation in inflation

Further moderation in inflation hangs on inflation psychology as much as labor market developments

Australian headline inflation has fallen over 4%-pt since the 4Q22 peak, and the unemployment rate has risen less than 0.5%-pt. Plotting the inflation rate against the unemployment rate suggests inflation has become more responsive to unemployment rate changes over the past few years (Figure 1). This is in keeping with many models of non-linear Phillips curves, which suggest nominal wage rigidities should give rise to greater sensitivity between inflation and the unemployment rate in tight labor markets. But it<sup>’</sup>s hard to pin down the relationship between inflation and unemployment via a simple plot. Movements in inflation may reflect supply shocks, while the NAIRU and inflation expectations may vary too.

In this note, we find recent movements in inflation do not appear to reflect a steeper Phillips curve and short-run inflation expectations better explain variation in inflation. Evidence of a flat Phillips curve implies the case for a hawkish rates outlook would be better founded upon still-elevated short-run inflation expectations, than the current state of the labor market. For the doves, weak activity data alone does not assure achievement of the inflation target, particularly given inflation expectations appear sensitive to items in the basket in which price growth remains strong.

Figure 1: Australian inflation vs. unemployment rate CPI growth, %oya
![](images/4ec513ee327faa99f03ec5f7d8503c3b7125edae9b5607ea78d9f5a8066f2ab9.jpg)

## Flat track

We can sidestep some of the endogeneity issues involved in estimating the slope of the Phillips curve by starting from the assumption that the Phillips curve really is flat, then examine whether a steeper curve would fit the recent data better. We adopt the approach used in Beaudry, Hou and Portier (BHP, 2023) and model quarterly headline inflation as a function of consumer inflation expectations and the current unemployment gap, proxied by the difference between the unemployment rate and the RBA<sup>’</sup>s estimate of NAIRU. We assume that pass-through from quarter-ahead consumer inflation expectations to headline inflation is just below one for one and that a 1%-pt unemployment gap only reduces quarterly headline inflation by 1.5bp, implying a very flat Phillips curve.<sup>1</sup>.

Figure 2: Phillips curve residual vs. unemployment gap Phillips Curve residual
![](images/d3ff304bf7d86cf45996debcea19a927645ed8d1dc5850d5f7fa3a397a41055d.jpg)

We generate Phillips curve residuals by taking the difference between actual inflation, and a forecast of inflation based on our specified Phillips curve. Figure 2 plots the residuals against the unemployment gap, and shows limited evidence of a significant relationship between the pair. If the specified Phillips curve was too flat, then we should observe a strong, negative relationship between the residuals and the unemployment gap. We do not observe this association, because the implied residuals/forecast misses have occurred in a pattern quite independent of the state of the labor market, which makes it hard to explain the recent bout of inflation via a fundamental steepening in the Phillips curve.

Figure 3: Phillips curve residual vs. vacancy to unemployment ratio Phillips Curve residual
![](images/c7abb4405b7dd035348b45bb08f2f77016461564f9ff0dde8eedcddfb4e07374.jpg)

Figure 3 shows that the same results hold if we use the negative of the vacancy to unemployment rate as our measure of labor market tightness. Greater labor market tightness (measured by a more negative vacancy to unemployment rate) is not strongly associated with larger Phillips curve forecast errors, so suggests a flat Phillips curve still fits the recent data relatively well.

## Weighty expectations

The results above prescribe only limited pass-through from labor market tightness to inflation, and instead suggest inflation expectations should play a larger role in driving price changes. We test this further by estimating a vector auto-regression between quarter-ahead consumer inflation expectations, quarterly headline inflation, and the unemployment gap. Figure 4 shows the impulse response of inflation to shocks in inflation expectations and the unemployment gap.<sup>2</sup>.

Figure 4: Impulse responses of quarterly inflation
![](images/84c00c0cc79bec179741fafcf7a8d212f3a5fe46525a612b25627bd3ab16cab3.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan

Quarterly headline inflation spikes 0.7%-pt on the impact of its own idiosyncratic shock, but the impulse response in the following quarters is much smaller. One interpretation is that inflation shocks are mostly akin to supply shocks which dissipate quickly after the initial impact. The peak response of inflation to an expectations shock is initially smaller, but its impact on inflation is more persistent over the year, compared to both the labor market shock and the idiosyncratic inflation shock. Consistent with a flat Phillips curve, the labor market shock only has a minimal impact on inflation, never adding more than 0.04%-pt to inflation in a single quarter across the forecast horizon.

What do the impulse responses say about the inflation process? Beaudry, Hou and Portier (2023) argue that, following a broad-based supply shock, agents infer the common component of inflation has increased and lift their short-run inflation expectations, driving more persistent inflation. When supply shocks are narrowly concentrated however, agents look through the movement in inflation and the effect is short lived. This explanation appears consistent with the shape of the impulse responses in the Australia data, with a temporary spike in inflation in response to an idiosyncratic inflation shock and a more persistent response following an expectations shock.

## Driving forces

If a large share of the recent inflation episode owes to a broad-based supply shock feeding through inflation expectations, then we should care about the evolution of inflation expectations from here as much as the state of the labor market when thinking about the return of inflation to target.

In prior work, we examined the drivers of inflation expectations by finding the weighted combination of inflation subgroups that best forecast year-ahead consumer expectations. We update that framework here, and run a LASSO regression of year-ahead consumer inflation expectations against the annual inflation rates of expenditure items in the CPI basket from 1Q00-1Q24. We interpret the regression coefficients as the weights that consumers place on different items in the basket, when forming their year-ahead inflation expectations.

Figure 5: Weight on CPI items in consumer inflation expectations
![](images/154b38a32b49cc6825e06702c5a8230fbe08b86fa3dcc6064d904dc4b8dbe6f0.jpg)

Figure 5 shows the three items with the highest and lowest LASSO coefficients. We do not constrain the coefficients to be positive, so the items can have a negative <sup>‘</sup>weight<sup>’</sup>, if consumers tend to revise down their expectation of year-ahead inflation in response to a rise in inflation in a particular item. Behavioural evidence suggests consumers tend to overweight salient price changes when forming expectations. In keeping with this, consumers weight food, fuel, and rent highly. These items are all essentials and either experience frequent price changes (food and fuel), or change less frequently but are personally observable (rent).

We can also think of the expectations series as representing consumers<sup>’</sup> forecast of year-ahead inflation. The negative coefficients might represent the fact some items lag headline inflation (e.g., due to indexation lags with alcohol prices and slower-moving services prices with recreation and culture) or are subject to short-lived supply chain shocks (e.g., audio-visual equipment), so tend to do a poor job of forecasting future inflation.

Figure 6: Actual vs. model forecast consumer inflation expectations Year-ahead consumer inflation expectations, %
![](images/a1bc0f08ac8b0656b8e959c84bd448e4954655058da8f949b23fe8edc161ce24.jpg)

The LASSO weights differ significantly from actual CPI weights, so the mix of the CPI data matter for inflation expectations as much as the level of headline. To that end, recent strength in rent and fuel prices presents upside risks to inflation expectations. That said, there is some evidence that consumers have been adjusting their implicit weightings over the recent bout of inflation, as actual expectations have consistently undershot model estimates (Figure 6).

## Test of faith

Evidence that inflation expectations are an important driver of inflation, independent of the state of the labor market, has a few implications for the outlook:

The RBA<sup>’</sup>s forecast for inflation to return to target without the unemployment rate moving materially above NAIRU is possible, so long as expectations are targetconsistent. Similarly, recent weakness in the activity data may not be sufficient to return inflation to target, if shortrun expectations remain elevated.

The distribution of inflation across items matters to the extent that it impacts expectations. <sup>‘</sup>Sticky<sup>’</sup> service inflation is a concern, if it represents changes in inflation psychology. But labor costs and service price growth tend to lag headline inflation, so tight labor markets and elevated wage growth are less of a concern, so long as inflation broadly continues to move toward the target band.

Monetary policy still matters, and tighter policy may be necessary to ensure long-run expectations remain anchored. We have found previously that when long-term expectations become de-anchored, this is almost entirely explained by changes in short-term expectations.

## United States

• Core CPI increased a still-firm 0.3% in April, though below the hotter readings in 1Q

Retail sales stumbled last month, and downward revisions paint a less boomy picture of the consumer

• April manufacturing production and May factory surveys remain listless

Homebuilding activity has trended lower; looking for mixed housing demand indicators next week

Both inflation and economic activity are looking a little softer at the start of the second quarter. The highly anticipated April CPI came out a smidgen below expectations, with both the headline and ex.-food and energy core indices increasing 0.3% on the month. Given this in conjunction with the other inflation data reported this week we now expect the Fed<sup>’</sup>s preferred inflation gauge, the core PCE index, will have increased 0.26% last month (or 2.8% over year-ago). If realized, this monthly outturn would still be above the Fed<sup>’</sup>s 2% target, but also the slowest monthly increase since last December, before inflation heated back up in the first quarter.

While inflation was a touch softer than expected last month, the April retail sales report was a more striking disappointment. The core control measure of retail sales fell 0.3% and estimates of prior months<sup>’</sup> spending growth were revised down, indicating less robust real consumer spending growth in recent months. This report contributed to us revising lower our tracking of 1Q GDP growth to 1.1% (the government<sup>’</sup>s first official estimate was 1.6%) and to lower our expectation for current-quarter GDP growth from 2.5% to 2.25%.

The week started off with another somewhat cautionary signal on the consumer, as the NY Fed reported that auto loan and credit card delinquency rates in 1Q were the highest since 2010-11. Even so, we remain sanguine on prospects for consumer spending growth as long as the labor market remains solid. The latest sign was encouraging: after jumping the prior week, this week<sup>’</sup>s report on initial claims for unemployment benefits partly reversed that jump, suggesting no abrupt change in the health of the jobs market.

At his most recent press conference, Chair Powell indicated three likely paths for monetary policy. One is continued inflation strength, which would leave policy on hold. The other two, which would prompt easing, were renewed confidence that inflation is on a path back to 2%, or unexpected softening in the labor market. This week<sup>’</sup>s news was perhaps a baby step toward renewed confidence, but we think more will be needed before the FOMC is comfortable with lowering the funds rate. We continue to look for a first ease in July.

## A bit cooler inflation

Consumer price inflation moderated in April after the unpleasant strength experienced in the first quarter. The headline CPI increased 0.3% last month, a tenth softer than expectations, with the year-ago figure edging down from 3.5% to 3.4%. The ex-food and energy core CPI increased an expected 0.3% last month (to higher precision up 0.292%), with the year-ago figure slipping from 3.8% to 3.6%, the lowest in three years (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Consumer prices
![](images/801bc3ad9b542bf825bba857c739374034e69c60cf3f5085fe74ebd17ef1cbfe.jpg)

The downside surprise on the headline figure was driven by weaker increases in prices for both food (0.0%) and energy (1.1%). Even through the stronger core inflation spell to start the year, food price inflation has remained mercifully tame, and over the past three months is up at only a 0.5% annual rate. Even food away from home, which has a greater labor cost influence, is up only at only a 2.9% rate over the last three months.

Energy prices had their third consecutive firm increase, boosted by a 2.8% increase in gasoline prices. The overall increase in energy prices, however, was held down by a 0.6% decline in household energy prices.

Figure 2: Core goods and core services CPI
![](images/4384ed5c349e1260375e73309f1487b3ee395e517d77a4edd0f932182d7c7af9.jpg)

In the core category, core goods prices declined 0.1%, while core services inflation moderated to 0.4% (Figure 2). Within core goods, you had the usual ups (apparel: 1.2%) and downs (vehicles: -0.8%). In core services, the monthly increase in

tenants<sup>’</sup> rent moderated to 0.35%, the lowest monthly increase in three years and not too far above the pre-pandemic trend. Owners<sup>’</sup> equivalent rent, on the other hand, has remained pesky, increasing 0.42% last month, only marginally below the outturns over the prior few months.

Core services ex-rent (“super-core<sup>”</sup>) was up 0.42% in April, which is firm in the absolute but softer than what was seen in 1Q. Within that category, vehicle insurance was firm again, up another 1.8% (but this CPI category isn<sup>’</sup>t used in the PCE measure). Away from insurance, there were less notable super-core developments, with trend-like increases in both medical care services (0.4%) and education services (0.2%).

The other April reports on producer and imported price inflation both surprised on the upside. Both the headline and core producer price index (PPI) increased 0.5% last month. Meanwhile, non-fuel import prices were up 0.7% (nsa) last month, and 0.9% over year ago.

## Retail spending looks a little softer now

The April retail sales report disappointed as the headline was flat on the month despite a sizable (3.1%) monthly increase in sales at gasoline stations. The closely watched control measure (which excludes gasoline, food, building materials, and autos) also came in below expectations, declining 0.3% last month. Moreover, there were downward revisions to prior months, which imply softer consumer spending for 1Q and less momentum heading into the current quarter (Figure 3).

![](images/25ba306f0a33b00fac32cd1153c2ac8a1eb19019c057b32b64444d302475cad4.jpg)

Factoring in a variety of related inputs, we are tracking a 0.4% monthly increase for real consumer spending in April, and now see consumer spending for the current quarter running around a 3%q/q, saar pace, down from the 3.3%ar we had forecast prior to this week<sup>’</sup>s release.

While consumer fundamentals broadly remain favorable, some cooling in the labor market and signs of rising stress in household finances (including rising credit card delinquencies, Figure 4) point to some further likely moderation in consumer spending in coming months, after a strong surge in 2H23.

Figure 4: New delinquent balances (30+ days) % of current balance
![](images/a18db7a1580f077d5030b4e583816fbbb9a7f4018df1d4b023d46872d823cf5b.jpg)

## Housing in a holding pattern

April housing starts grew 5.7% in April, but part of that gain came off a downwardly revised level for starts the month prior; overall, starts in April ran at 1.36mn saar (Figure 5). Stepping back, the housing market has broadly stabilized for much of the past year, but prior hopes for an early rebound are not evident in the data. The move back up in mortgage rates in recent months may be acting as a bit of a drag on the sector, and supply remains limited.

Figure 5: Housing starts and permits
![](images/94709b90e83aee2d379d49c671bbbdf6cea3b4241e41121ea6a22b880ef476f4.jpg)

Housing will be front and center next week, in what is otherwise a relatively light week for economic data. We look for mixed changes in the April home sales data, with resales up and new home sales expected down. In the April durable goods report we look for another humdrum month for capital goods orders and shipments.

We think that initial jobless claims declined by 7,000 to 215,000 during the week ending May 18. Part of the surge in initial claims seen during the week ending May 4 was due to noise around spring break (and the Easter and Passover holidays) in certain states, but much of that normalized in the last print with a 10,000 decline. We see scope for some residual noise that might still add marginally to claims in the upcoming release from this distortion. That said, we expect initial claims to remain around historically low levels, underlying the relatively healthy state of the labor market.

We estimate that new orders for durable goods fell 0.7% in April, reversing much of the 0.9% rise in March (after recent revisions). While industry data suggest a decline in the volatile aircraft orders category that follows a surge during the prior two months, we look for weakness to extend more broadly across capital goods orders—with defense a notable exception—given more downbeat signals from a range of manufacturing sector data of late. Away from transportation, we look for new orders to have declined 0.4% in April with a similar-sized decline in core capital goods orders (which exclude defense and aircraft). For the shipments data, we expect another flat reading in April with a 0.3% rise in core capital goods shipments that follows recent declines.

Index (nsa)</td><td>Mar 79.4</td><td>Apr 77.2</td><td>May 67.4</td><td>May 68.0</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>82.5</td><td>79.0</td><td>68.8</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>77.4</td><td>76.0</td><td>66.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>2.9</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.5</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>2.8</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td></td></tr></table>

We look for the University of Michigan consumer sentiment index to be revised up slightly to 68.0, following a 9.8pt plunge in the preliminary May release. The broader trend in the sentiment data has been improving despite the big drop in the preliminary index for this month—the prior four months were all in the upper 70s, which had been the highest level since summer 2021—and most recent data have been relatively favorable even as consumers remain fairly downbeat. Specifically, the combination of a rise in stock prices with a fall in retail gasoline prices for the month to date should be supportive. News reports about April inflation this week also are likely to sound more positive for continued cooling (which could also weigh on the preliminary jump in inflation expectations for May). By contrast, the modest climb in jobless claims this month seems to largely be an artifact of unusual seasonal effects; the labor market remains firm. We therefore expect to see the big drop recorded in the preliminary report to look not quite as large in the final release, but still down materially from April.

The April producer price index (PPI) surprised to the upside, with both the headline and core rising 0.5% on the month, although there were downward revisions to some prior months. Energy was up notably on the month (2.0%), broadly as expected, while food unexpectedly contracted on the month (-0.7%). Excluding food and energy, the core reading was the firmest since last July. On a year-ago basis, headline PPI inflation firmed to 2.2%oya while core PPI inflation held steady at 2.4%. Within the core, final demand services rose 0.6% in April while final demand goods were up 0.3%—both firmer than anticipated—whereas construction rose a softerthan-expected 0.1%.

This report has limited direct implications for the April CPI release, as there are meaningful differences between the PPI (which measures prices received by firms for sales beyond just consumers) and CPI (which includes things like taxes paid and imported items that are not included in the PPI). Some “pipeline pressures<sup>”</sup> can show up in PPI first and then gradually translate into consumer prices over time; likely more relevant in the near-term are broad macro dynamics that suggest some cooling in an economy that is still running above potential and with a still tight labor market. Some PPI measures do feed directly into core PCE tracking estimates; we note that financial services were strong while airfares point to a contraction; medical costs were moderate overall.

Retail sales (May 15)

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

The April retail sales report disappointed as the headline was flat on the month despite a sizable (3.1%) monthly increase in sales at gasoline stations. The closely-watched control measure (which excludes gasoline, food, building materials, and autos) also came in below expectations, declining -0.3% last month. Moreover, there were downward revisions to prior months, which imply softer consumer spending for 1Q and less momentum heading into the current quarter. Factoring in a variety of related inputs, we are tracking a 0.4% monthly increase for real consumer spending in April, and now see consumer spending for the current quarter running around a 3%q/q saar pace, down from the 3.3%ar we had forecast prior to this release. That implies slightly slower, but still abovetrend , real GDP growth of 2.3%q/q saar for 2Q. The downward revisions to prior months, particularly February, lead us to look for 1Q GDP growth to be revised down to 1.2%ar from our 1.4% prior tracking. (The revisions to factory goods barely moved the needle on GDP tracking for last quarter.) While consumer fundamentals broadly remain favorable, some cooling in the labor market and signs of rising stress among lower-income households (including rising credit card delinquencies) point to some further likely moderation in consumer spending in coming months, after a strong surge in 2H23.

In the details of the report there was notable variation across sales categories in April (as is often the case), with six of thirteen categories recording an increase in nominal sales last month. As noted above, gasoline station sales led the list of gainers, followed by clothing (1.6%) and electronics (1.5%). Notable declines were reported for non-store (i.e., internet) retailers (-1.2%) and sporting goods (-0.9%). The one insight we receive from the report for the services sector, sales at eating and drinking establishments, increased a modest 0.2%.

CPI (May 15)
<table><tr><td>m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Core</td><td>0.36</td><td>0.36</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.29</td></tr><tr><td>%oya (nsa)</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.8</td><td>37</td><td>3.6</td></tr><tr><td>Core services</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.5</td><td></td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Core goods</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td></td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Food</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>申</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Energy</td><td>2.3</td><td>1.1</td><td>16</td><td>1.1</td></tr><tr><td>Housing</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Owners&#x27; eq.rent</td><td>0.44</td><td>0.44</td><td>0.41</td><td>0.42</td></tr><tr><td>Rent</td><td>0.46</td><td>0.41</td><td>0.39</td><td>0.35</td></tr><tr><td>Lodging away from home</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.1</td><td>-04</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Apparel</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.7</td><td>伸</td><td>1.2</td></tr><tr><td>New vehicles</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Used vehicles</td><td>0.5</td><td>-1.1</td><td>伸</td><td>-1.4</td></tr><tr><td>Public transportation</td><td>2.3</td><td>-1.0</td><td>申</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>Communication</td><td>0.5</td><td>-0.1</td><td>00</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Medical care</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.4</td><td></td></tr></table>

Consumer price inflation moderated in April after the unpleasant strength experienced in the first quarter. The overall Consumer Price Index (CPI) increased 0.3% last month, a tenth softer than expectations, with the year-ago figure edging down from 3.5% to 3.4%. The ex-food and energy core CPI increased an expected 0.3% last month (to higher precision up 0.292%), with the year-ago figure slipping from 3.8% to 3.6%, the lowest in three years. This number, as well as the PPI, imply a 0.23% increase in the April core PCE index, by our estimation, which would take the year-ago gain down from 2.8% to 2.7%. The April inflation outturns are still firmer than the Fed<sup>’</sup>s inflation goals, but at least are moving in the right direction again after the backsliding seen over the prior few months. We<sup>’</sup>re sticking with our call for a first Fed rate cut in July, but we probably need to see some further cooling in labor market activity for that to play out.

The downside surprise on the headline figure was driven by weaker increases in prices for both food (0.0%) and energy (1.1%). Even through the stronger core inflation spell to start the year, food price inflation has remained mercifully tame, and over the past three months is up at only a 0.5% annual rate. Even food away from home, which has a greater labor cost influence, is up only at only a 2.9% rate over the last three months. Energy prices had their third consecutive firm increase, boosted by a 2.8% increase in gasoline prices. The overall increase in energy prices, however, was held down by a 0.6% decline in household energy prices. In the core category, core goods prices declined 0.1%, while core service inflation moderated to 0.4%. Within core goods, we had the usual ups (apparel: 1.2%) and downs (vehicles: -0.8%). In core services, the monthly increase in tenants<sup>’</sup> rent moderated to 0.35%, the lowest monthly increase in three years and not too far above the pre-pandemic trend. Owners<sup>’</sup> equivalent rent, on the other hand, has remained pesky, increasing 0.42% last month, only marginally below the outturns over the prior few months. Core services ex-rent (super-core) was up 0.42% in

April, which is firm in the absolute but softer than what was seen in 1Q. Within that category, vehicle insurance was firm again, up another 1.8% (this CPI category isn<sup>’</sup>t used in the PCE measure). Away from insurance, there were less notable super-core developments, with trend-like increases in both medical care services (0.4%) and education services (0.2%).

Against expectations (by consensus and us) for an improvement from April, the Empire State manufacturing survey slid to -15.6 from -14.3 last month and has been in contractionary territory for the past six months now. Details were mixed as shipments rebounded in May while new orders slipped; employment also declined but hours worked improved. Both prices paid and prices received eased this month. In addition, capex intentions fell to their lowest level in a year, remaining just positive and well off elevated levels earlier in the pandemic recovery. The Empire State survey has remained volatile since the pandemic began, which makes us more cautious about taking much signal from it. That said, the ISM-weighted composite improved to a still-weak 46.9 in May (from 46.0).

## Homebuilders survey (May 15)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Housing market</td><td>51</td><td>51</td><td>51</td><td>45</td></tr><tr><td>Present sales</td><td>56</td><td>57</td><td></td><td>51</td></tr><tr><td>Prospective buyer traffic</td><td>34</td><td>35 34</td><td></td><td>30</td></tr></table>

The NAHB homebuilders<sup>’</sup> survey surprised lower, dropping back to 45 from 51 in April—its first decline in six months and its lowest level since January. All three components of the survey fell with the largest drop in the expectations component; prospective buyers<sup>’</sup> traffic remains well below its prepandemic average and its post-pandemic rebound through early 2022. Although mortgage rates have eased some in recent weeks, they remain in the mid-7% range and may be acting as a renewed drag on housing market activity after some stabilization and even modest improvement in some measures from late last year.

## Housing starts (May 16)

<table><tr><td colspan="6">Million units, saar</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td></tr><tr><td>Starts</td><td>1.55</td><td></td><td>1.32</td><td>1.29 135</td><td>1.36</td></tr><tr><td>Single-family starts</td><td>1.17</td><td>1.13</td><td>1.02 1.03</td><td>11</td><td>1.03</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts</td><td>0.38</td><td>0.41</td><td>0.30 0.25</td><td>0.34</td><td>0.33</td></tr><tr><td>Permits</td><td>1.52</td><td>1.56</td><td>1.47</td><td>1.48 145</td><td>1.44</td></tr></table>

The annualized pace of April housing starts and permits was largely in line with expectations, although revisions resulted in larger-than-expected monthly swings. April housing starts grew 5.7% in April, but part of that gain came off a downwardly revised level for starts the month prior; overall, starts in April ran at 1.36mn saar, very close to our forecast. As expected, permits slid on the month, down -3.0% to a 1.44mn pace—also close to our forecast. The details confirmed a very small decline in single-family starts, whereas the much more volatile multifamily category surged to mostly reverse a large decline in March. Permits, by contrast, saw a modest decline in single-family and a large drop in multifamily. Overall, the housing market has broadly stabilized for much of the past year, but prior hopes for an early rebound are not evident in the data. The move back up in mortgage rates in recent months may be acting as a bit of a drag on the sector, and supply still remains limited.

## Import prices (May 16)

<table><tr><td>%m/m, nsa, unless noted</td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Import prices</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-0.9</td><td></td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>0.4</td><td>1.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex-fuel import prices</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>00</td><td>0.7</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>04</td><td>0.9</td></tr></table>

The import price index surprised higher in April, rising 0.9% to help lift the year-ago inflation rate to 1.1%oya—the highest reading since end-2022. While higher imported petroleum prices contributed to this rise, non-fuel import prices were also firmer than expected, gaining 0.7% for April to help raise their year-ago pace to 0.9%oya; ex-food and fuels were up 0.6% on the month to 0.4%oya. Given that import prices are not seasonally adjusted, the year-ago inflation readings may be more informative, and suggest that after a period of deflation in imported goods prices helping to pull down consumer price measures—non-fuel import prices declined -0.8%oya

last December—more recent readings are consistent with that disinflationary pressure on goods prices disappearing, and perhaps even moving into positive territory. Looking at some of the details of the report, import prices for consumer goods ex autos were nearly flat on the month (up 0.1%), while imported auto prices rose 0.3%. That helped lift the year-ago inflation rate for imported autos to 2.6%.

The import price index is also the last input this month into our core PCE tracking estimate for April. The report marginally lifts that estimate, and we now see core PCE prices rising 0.26% last month, with the year-ago inflation rate holding steady at March<sup>’</sup>s 2.8%oya.
