The Philadelphia Fed manufacturing survey<sup>’</sup>s headline declined from its highest level in two years at 15.5 in April to 4.5 in May, disappointing expectations. Most current indicators declined in the May reading including new orders (to -7.9 from 12.2 in April) and shipments (to -1.2 from 19.1 in April). A notable exception was the employment index, which remained weak at -7.9 albeit improving a touch relative to April<sup>’</sup>s level of -10.7. We have seen some mixed survey results lately but this print seems consistent with the latest reading from the Empire State manufacturing survey, which was also below expectations.

Also, we received the latest reading from NY Fed<sup>’</sup>s Business Leaders Survey for May, which edged higher, indicating increased activity in the region<sup>’</sup>s services sector. The headline measure increased 4pts to 3, its highest level in over a year.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Industrial production</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.8</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.1</td><td>01</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>1.2</td><td>1.4</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.2</td><td>02</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Motor vehicles &amp; parts</td><td>3.4</td><td>4.9</td><td>3.1</td><td>2.8</td><td>07</td><td>-2.0</td></tr><tr><td>High-tech</td><td>-44</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>1.0</td></tr><tr><td>Mfg ex motor vehicles</td><td>1.0</td><td>1.1</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.0</td><td>02</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Business equipment</td><td>2.0</td><td>1.7</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.2</td><td></td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Capacity utilization (%,sa)</td><td>78.2</td><td>78.5</td><td>78.4</td><td>78.5</td><td>78.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Manufacturing</td><td>77.1 77.2</td><td></td><td>77.4 77.2</td><td></td><td>77.4</td><td>76.9</td></tr></table>

Industrial production was flat in April, with manufacturing output disappointing by contracting -0.3% on the month. Meanwhile utilities jumped 2.8% whereas mining slipped -0.6% in April, both broadly as expected. Within the manufacturing measure, auto production declined -2.0% (more than anticipated) and non-motor vehicle production surprised with a modest -0.1% contraction on the month. The report was also accompanied by downward revisions to March, and together these data suggest somewhat less momentum in the manufacturing sector and represent a near-term challenge to our expectations for a broader recovery in industrial output despite signs of shrinking inventories and still-firm goods demand. We would feel more comfortable with the expected rebound if the related survey data pointed more consistently toward expansion.

## Focus: Minimum wage changes in California

California raised the binding minimum wage for fast food workers to \$20 an hour starting on April 1. The statewide minimum wage has been \$16 an hour since January 1, 2024, for all employees covered by the law. Later next month, a separate law will start a phased minimum wage hike for some healthcare workers, bringing the minimum hourly wage to \$25 eventually. Assuming that all of the impacted workers were paid at or near the state minimum wage prior to these increases, both of these changes represent large wage increases in the most populous US state. Hence, these changes raised some concerns about potential upside to national wage inflation numbers. Our analysis suggests that the direct impact on aggregate average hourly earnings from each of these changes would be small. It was roughly around 2bp in April from the change impacting fast food workers and would be another 4bp in June from the upcoming raise for healthcare workers.

Given the size of the impacted workforce relative to aggregate employment, the phased implementation of the upcoming change for healthcare workers and the presence of many jurisdictions with effectively higher minimum wages than the state level minimum wage in CA, the estimated impact is relatively small on macro aggregates. Theory suggests that minimum wage increases would also lead to reduced hours and slower employment growth, but the evidence on those margins is mixed and may be less relevant for large increases specific to individual sectors like the aforementioned cases in CA.

Figure 1: Average hourly earnings in leisure and hospitality
![](images/16584db9a5096679de39fef4d3cccea8f842650138864b210f8cd7e1c02c8d28.jpg)

According to the latest data from QCEW that cover the first three quarters of 2023, roughly around 560,000 workers were employed in limited service restaurants in the state, approximately a quarter of all employment in leisure and hospitality in CA. Since there are provisions in the legislation to allow for phased implementation by facility type, location, employer size, or other employer characteristics, how many workers out of roughly 2.8 million healthcare workers are covered is hard to quantify. A recent study by UC Berkeley Labor Center estimates that roughly 320,000 workers are covered who are earning below the required minimum, with a an average increase of about 19% with the implementation of the new law in June.

Figure 2: Average hourly earnings in education and health
![](images/ca0a54a6269394920d57ab8d06bd9c018dca73d68332d0449649353992d77092.jpg)

Leisure and hospitality recovered more slowly relative to the aggregate economy, both in the US and in the state of CA. In fact, as of March (last month for the state payroll data) employment level in the leisure and hospitality sector, in CA, was still 1.3% below February 2020 level. By contrast, aggregate leisure and hospitality employment in the US fully recovered to its respective pre-pandemic level by April. The opposite is true for education and health, as employment levels are currently 12.6% and 6.6% above where they were in February 2020, for CA and the US, respectively. State-level wage gains also differed from national trends somewhat for these two sectors. Average hourly earnings in leisure and hospitality grew much more slowly in CA over the past year relative to the aggregate trend in the sector (Figure 1). Wage growth for education and healthcare workers in CA tracked the national trend more closely (Figure 2).

Figure 3: CA state minimum wage relative to AHE in different sectors % 2024 AHE is the average of monthly data ytd., MW includes changes in 2024
![](images/629ac193dd93283911b0b2e7b4644afd01340177b7d17dc05f8f5135ed7c68c0.jpg)

Both of these minimum wage changes represent a large increase relative to average wages in the respective industries (Figure 3). These large changes increase the possibility of more adjustments along the hours and employment margin as well, and the empirical research on this issue provides less guidance in such large increases in specific sectors.

## Euro Area

• GDP and employment grew similarly in 1Q24, implying zero productivity growth

This biases unit labor cost growth higher, but wages and the GDP deflator have slowed a bit

• Euro area final HICP confirms sticky services inflation

• Despite this, ECB remains on track for a June cut

As has been the case for quite a few weeks now, ECB Governing Council members are firmly pointing to a first rate cut in June, even though incoming data show an improvement in growth and further stickiness in inflation. The stickiness in inflation is concentrated in services, which the ECB has highlighted as particularly important. But, the ECB has also likely had enough encouragement from signs of some moderation in wage growth to see its disinflation forecast on track in a broad sense.

In terms of growth, this week<sup>’</sup>s data confirmed the pickup in 1Q24 GDP growth and revealed a solid 1.2%q/q saar rise in employment, which left output per worker stagnant in 1Q. That is better than the outright contraction seen last year, but still biases unit labor costs upwards. Next week, we expect the Euro area composite PMI to reinforce these trends. We expect the composite output index to increased by another 0.3pt to 52.0, which would be consistent with a 1.4%ar on GDP growth and we suspect that this would be matched by a further increase in the employment index, which keeps productivity growth at zero. The composite output index would also confirm the upside risk we have been flagging around our 1%ar growth forecast.

In terms of inflation, this week<sup>’</sup>s report confirmed the flash release. Core goods prices fell in April by a surprisingly large 0.17%m/m, sa, while services prices rose by a solid 0.32%m/ m when the timing of Easter is considered. At this stage, services inflation looks sticky and there are only limited signs in the data of the slowing we have in our forecast. But, with wage growth seemingly coming off its peak and recent services inflation strength likely having rotated to a possibly more narrow set of categories, we still expect some slowing ahead. The exact timing is however uncertain.

## Growth remains very job-intensive

The second estimate of GDP confirmed the flash estimate, leaving GDP up by more in 1Q24 than the 0.5%q/q saar signalled by the PMI. Some of this may relate to mild weather, but national data also suggest a broader improvement, including in domestic demand. For April, the PMI was signalling a further pick-up in underlying growth momentum to near 1.3%ar, which implies upside risk to our near-term forecast.

By country, Spain stands out on the upside in 1Q24 with growth of 2.9%q/q saar, while the other large economies in the region all performed similarly and close to the Euro area average. No details were published with this second estimate of Euro area GDP.

Figure 1: Euro area employment
%q/q saar, model estimated over pre-2008 period
![](images/c2ab4605f9d0c69eb4b23cfb9889c77178d24be0547334aac1636d3c22f61a2e.jpg)

The only new information was a first estimate of employment in 1Q24 (Figure 1). With real GDP up 1.3%q/q saar and employment up 1.2%q/q saar in 1Q24, output per worker was essentially stagnant in the quarter. This is better than the outright declines at a 1.1%ar over the prior year, but is still weak (Figure 2). The PMI does not signal much improvement as the composite employment index has also lifted further recently.

Figure 2: Euro area labour productivity
![](images/0707843e7f87a0247487b8a857c5e32b9080fbfad18eb4eab8264d59e16db187.jpg)

For the ECB, this remains a key pressure point as it has been expecting a strong pick-up in productivity this year that allows unit labour cost growth to soften relative to the growth of wages. There is no sign of this. At the same time, there are signs in the national reports that wage growth is starting to turn lower and that the GDP deflator is moderating somewhat, which could point to some inflationary relief from unit profits. Hence, the disappointment on productivity is arguably being offset by progress in other areas, which keeps the ECB on track to cut rates in June. But, some improvement in productivity will likely have to materialize quite soon to support the disinflation process.

## Euro area IP improves (a bit)

The manufacturing sector has underperformed the broader economy in recent quarters. March has brought some improvement however, with Euro area IP up 0.6%m/m in total and 0.9%m/m in manufacturing. This left both down sharply in 1Q24, albeit with large drags from the notoriously volatile and distorted Irish data. On an ex-Ireland basis, tota IP fell 0.5%m/m and manufacturing output fell 0.4%m/m in March. But, the latter reversed only half of February<sup>’</sup>s gain and left 1Q24 down only modestly by 0.5%q/q saar (Figure 3). This likely helped the 1Q24 GDP and we note that the German VDA data are showing a solid improvement in car production in April. However, the PMI is still sending a more cautious message, despite some recent improvement. Hence, our focus is on next<sup>’</sup>s week<sup>’</sup>s flash PMI for May for a better steer on the sector<sup>’</sup>s performance.

Figure 3: Euro area manufacturing output and PMI
![](images/347fbc2b723e9d5635810fb59d3b4572ee20efa142682d346da2107e83478b2f.jpg)

## Services inflation still sticky

It was already known from the flash report that Euro area services inflation had gained some momentum so far this year (figure 4). But, the key for the final HICP release was on the services details and the extent to which Easter has played a role in recent outturns.

Figure 4: Euro area services inflation
![](images/6ff70a08a9566fc7a8f0816e084dbbf3a663dcceab69aac7e557c890789d9cfa.jpg)

In the event, it seems that Easter likely had a relatively modest effect and that this averaged out over March and April, with only a modest possibly upward effect likely in May.

Only airfares have exhibited the normal Easter-related profile, whereas recent changes in package holiday price inflation and hotel price inflation have been quite modest (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Euro area tourism inflation
![](images/2128021e9b954d83e20b809dfd6a38036a4010d23cac1d8894195d14d4eb6bb9.jpg)

This leaves services inflation looking sticky, whereas core goods have surprised on the downside in March and April with outright declines. What seems to have happened recently on the services side is that pressures have rotated away from tourism-related items towards a new set that includes insurance prices and some administered prices (Figure 6). The German VAT hike on restaurant meals in January has also had an effect.

Figure 6: Euro area insurance and German restaurant inflation %oya
![](images/462d27931c982934e5a3b636e2ea28e9831ff73f6a6109ae8ae62ef830f5e98c.jpg)

Our forecast still has a moderation in services inflation starting in May. That wage growth appears to be turning is encouraging in that regard. Similarly, the rotation in inflation pressures may also point to some moderation ahead. But, of course, the timing of this is hard to gauge. In terms of core inflation, the surprises have been much smaller given the outright declines in core goods prices. But, we will need to see stronger evidence of a moderation in services inflation to validate our broader call that Euro area inflation can moderate back to the ECB<sup>’</sup>s 2% target without a pronounced weakening in the economy and labor market.

# Data releases and forecasts Week of May 20 - 24

Output and surveys

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Construction output</td><td></td><td>Jan</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td></tr><tr><td>Tue</td><td>Euro area</td><td>Dec</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>May 21</td><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td>1.8</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>2.7</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.5</td><td></td></tr></table>

Available national data suggest that Euro area construction may have gained another 0.5%m/m in March. Earlier improvements were likely assisted by mild weather, while a further gain in March could indicate some underlying resilience, including to higher rates. If confirmed, it would lead construction output up around 7%q/q saar in 1Q24.

The Euro area composite PMI has gained 4pts over the past three releases. The combination of this strong upward momentum, and that parts of the survey have improved from very low starting points, suggest scope for further gains. However, the PMI in April was already consistent with a 1.3%ar on GDP growth, which exceeds our 1%ar forecast for the rest of this year. To balance these considerations, we have pencilled in a further increase, albeit a modest one. If confirmed, it would leave the PMI signalling a 1.4%ar on GDP growth and hence signal further upside risk to our forecast.

At the sector level, we expect the improvement to come from manufacturing, which has lagged the recovery. Admittedly, the details in manufacturing were mixed in April and hence an increase is far from certain in May. At the country level, we expect the improvement to come from Germany and France, which have lagged behind the rest of the region. This would close the performance gaps.

Another aspect to watch is the employment index. The ECB expects a solid pickup in productivity, but the PMI employment index has increased alongside the output index recently. Hence, the composition of the PMI has continued to point to weak output per worker.

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Consumer confidence (prelim)</td><td colspan="3"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Thu</td><td>Euro area (European Commission survey)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>May 23</td><td>% balance of responses</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>11:00am Consumer confidence</td><td>-15.5 -14.9</td><td>-14.7</td><td>-14.0</td></tr></table>

The improvement in Euro area consumer confidence slowed a bit in April, but we think that real income improvements and declines in inflation perceptions are still coming through and should support confidence. The strength of the labor market argues in the same direction. As a result, we have pencilled in a somewhat larger increase for May.

The German economy improved in 1Q24 according to the flash print of 0.9%q/q saar. We expect this to be confirmed in the final report. The business surveys have also improved, which suggests a return to positive growth in an underlying sense into 2Q. In contrast, the 1Q24 outcome was likely still supported by temporary effects, especially the mild weather. The full report will give a better sense of Germany<sup>’</sup>s recent

Industrial production

performance and of its drivers. One key area is capex, which weakened in 4Q23. Another area is wages and the labor input variables, especially for signs of where this leaves labor costs and the inflation outlook. Germany is likely to have seen a delayed pickup in labor costs, reflecting the delayed pay increases in recent settlements.

## Japan

• 1Q24 GDP deepened the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy dilemma, with consumption falling for the fourth consecutive quarter

• The weak yen is posing downside risks for consumption, through lingering real income drags

• The BoJ likely will be required to balance providing monetary accommodation with controlling the yen

We think this week’s reduction in JGB purchase was a signal for a decision of a start of QT, likely in June

The BoJ laid the foundation for an upcoming QT decision this week. The BoJ has indicated a policy of continuing JGB purchases at about the JPY6 trillion per month, and had kept the subsequent purchases unchanged since the change in policy framework in March. But this week, the BoJ surprised the market by reducing the purchase for the first time since then. We have long argued that the BoJ has a strong incentive to start QT early, given the size of its balance sheet. As all relevant opinions by policy board members at the April policy meeting supported a decision to start QT in the near future, we expect the BoJ to officially announce it at the June meeting ahead of an additional rate hike in July.

The BoJ policy dilemma is getting deeper. While the BoJ plans to support a recovery of domestic demand through maintaining its policy accommodation, the weaker yen caused by the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s delay in policy normalization is increasingly causing a downward pressure on domestic consumption through rising expectations for lingering real income drags. This week<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 real GDP report made this concern a reality, falling 2%q/q, saar. About half of the decline was due to transitory factors, such as the closure of auto plants. But consumption falling for the fourth consecutive quarter is posing challenges for the BoJ. Sluggish domestic demand does not support additional rate hikes, but excessive delays in the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s policy normalization could further drag on domestic consumption through pressures on the yen. Balancing these risks, we expect the BoJ to proceed with policy normalization, through an early start of QT and gradual rate hikes, even when growth is lacking strength.

## 1Q GDP contracted

The Cabinet Office<sup>’</sup>s first estimate of 1Q24 GDP was disappointing, falling 2.0%q/q, saar. 4Q23 growth was also revised down, from 0.4% to 0%. Japan<sup>’</sup>s economy has been contracting since 2H23, after having bounced sharply in 1H23. Both domestic and net external demand were weak in 1Q, dragging overall growth by 1.5%-pt each, partly offset by a positive contribution from inventories (Figure 1). Despite the very accommodative monetary policy settings, Japan<sup>’</sup>s economy has performed weakly, with households<sup>’</sup> real income growth having remained negative for two years in a row. In more detail:

Private consumption fell 2.7%q/q saar, marking the fourth consecutive quarterly decline (Figure 2). We had expected negative consumption growth in 1Q, and pointed to a further downside risk to our forecasts based on the monthly real consumption data. Despite some recovery in consumer sentiment during the quarter, reflecting a trend decline in inflation and rising wage prospects, consumers appear to have yet to become confident in increasing their spending by drawing down their savings. While we continue to expect some consumption recovery, with a gradual fading of the real income drags, we now expect a weaker recovery. We now expect real consumption to recover modestly toward the year-end, reaching a level modestly below that in 4Q23.

Capex also disappointed, falling 3.2%q/q, saar. However, given the quarterly volatility in GDP-based capex, we think this was a partial payback from a strong 7.4% jump in 4Q. Business sentiment remained solid backed by favorable corporate revenue. We continue to expect fixed investment to trend higher, as the backdrop of ongoing labor shortages likely will catalyze a shift higher in firms capital expenditure.

Both exports and imports of goods and services marked double-digit declines, falling 18.7%q/q, saar, and 12.8%, respectively. We had expected a large drop in imports, but the actual decline was larger in exports. The large drop in exports was largely attributable to the factory shutdowns by an automaker as well as the Noto earthquake in the start of the quarter. We expect a strong bounce in exports in coming quarters. Given the recent weakening of the yen, we expect the export recovery to outpace that in imports, resulting in net exports contributing positively to overall growth over the rest of the year.

Figure 1: Contribution to real GDP growth
![](images/57efac3b4829f2286cd915ec62f21e85ce233941bd5ab00547b5724675c5e6a3.jpg)

Figure 2: Real private consumption and compensation of employees %q/q, saar
![](images/052f152031be336c03ee83bc2088254bc1ef4ce01627483b9e94edbe31154d18.jpg)

The 1Q GDP print indicated rising downside risks to Japan<sup>’</sup>s 2024 growth, particularly for domestic consumption. We expect production and exports to recover in the coming quarters, as their 1Q weakness was caused by one-off special factors. However, despite rising prospects for higher nominal wages, households appear to have yet to gain confidence in increasing consumption, with rising fears of sticky inflation. As the recent trend decline in inflation likely will reverse in 2Q, reflecting the scheduled removal of the government<sup>’</sup>s energy subsidy, consumers likely will remain cautious about increasing spending. We now expect 2024 GDP growth to be -0.2%, with 4Q/4Q growth to rise a modest 0.3%.

1. Domestic private sector, ex for ships and from utilities

We look for a pullback in monthly core private domestic orders in March after a large rise the prior month.

## Canada

• We expect core inflation decelerated in April

• We are sticking with our call that the Bank of Canada will cut the policy rate in June

• Housing starts declined in April and builders<sup>’</sup> sentiment remains somber

Next week the April CPI report will be released, the last inflation report the Bank of Canada will see before the June 5 monetary policy announcement. Our headline CPI forecast is 0.2%sa, which translates to 2.7% on a year-over-year basis following 2.9%oya in March. Core CPI (excluding food and energy) is also estimated to have increased 0.2%sa last month or 2.7%oya, matching the headline annual pace. Similarly, the expected annual pace in the core is softer than the 2.9%oya increase in March. We expect some of the bounce up in the trend pace of services CPI will be reversed in April (Figure 1), although some special cost dynamics were triggered last month (discussed below). If we are right we expect the deceleration in the core CPI will be seen favorably by the Bank of Canada, so we continue to place a high probability on a policy rate cut at the June meeting. We are sticking with our call that the BOC will start lowering rates next month. All the rhetoric following the April monetary policy announcement—the monetary policy brief, Governor<sup>’</sup>s Macklem<sup>’</sup>s opening statement, and the press conference—was tinged with dovish commentary. The language around the evolution of underlying inflation was generally more constructive compared with the March brief and the Bank was encouraged by the progress seen in 2024. The further gradual easing in inflation should be welcome news at the Bank and provide enough of the hard evidence that the Bank has been seeking that the downward momentum in underlying inflation is sustainable.

Figure 2: CPI-Shelter
![](images/9bfabf0ac9e5435c976a6c92781f888853e3a1d63b6a717844c6f3496aed2a25.jpg)
Source:Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan Global Economics

Headline CPI will be boosted (again) by higher gasoline prices in April. An annual adjustment in minimum wage rates across all the Atlantic provinces took effect on April 1 and we expect the change may boost monthly costs for food purchased from restaurants and household operations. In addition the federal government officially raised its alcohol excise tax on April 1 to 2%. We expect easing price gains in other categories will more than offset those irregular cost pressures.

## Nonstarter

The annualized pace of housing starts declined for a second month in April. Housing starts have slid a total of 7.6% since February largely on a decline in the multifamily category (which tends to be more volatile) with single starts about stable. Overall, housing starts activity has decelerated materially after some stronger growth through mid-2023 (Figure 2). Higher mortgage rates appear to be acting as a drag on the sector. The Bank of Canada raised the policy rate in June 2023 and again in July 2023 to a terminal rate of 5.0%. Housing supply has been struggling to keep up with housing demand. The recent weakness will keep upward pressure on home prices and rents and make housing affordability challenging.

Figure 2: Housing starts
![](images/d4ca76b20188860d0bcc8845bb74d28bcee8e71d35fe242686e676e7ce66c205.jpg)

For a seventh consecutive quarter, the Canadian Home Builders<sup>’</sup> Association Housing Market Index (HMI) showed very negative home builder sentiment. While there was some improvement over sentiment in 4Q23 the HMI remained low, pointing to lower starts in the year ahead. An expectation of easing monetary policy factored into builders<sup>’</sup> slightly improved assessment of current and future sales conditions in 1Q24. The consensus view among the home builders surveyed is that the Bank of Canada will begin to lower interest rates at the June or July policy announcements. Higher interest rates continued to weigh on home construction activity—65% of builders said that the level of interest rates has caused them to build fewer units than they otherwise would and 31% stated they have canceled projects for the same reason. A majority stated that they expect their housing starts in 2024 to be below their level of starts in 2023 with just 15% of all respondents expected to have more starts in 2024 than 2023.

## Mexico

• Minutes to unveil several shades of hawkishness

• Services CPI strength should show true colors in May

Final 1Q GDP and March data to set track to 2Q bounce

• Manufacturing rebound still in sight

The risks to our macroeconomic base case have become more one-sided, but visibility is limited as to their magnitude. Next week should help clarify the picture to a good degree, at least in the short term (2Q). Focus will be on Banxico<sup>’</sup>s minutes, but May preliminary CPI data and a handful of late-1Q and early-2Q activity indicators will also be of first-order importance. The statement accompanying Banxico<sup>’</sup>s widely expected decision to keep rates on hold just two months after delivering the first cut in the current cycle was hawkish.

The Bank unanimously agreed to hold rates, significantly revised up this year<sup>’</sup>s inflation forecasts for headline and core CPI, and delayed the convergence of inflation to target to the end of next year instead of by 2H24. Most importantly, it acknowledged that this was largely motivated by its expectations for stickier core services inflation.

The minutes should reflect this hawkish shift, but offer different degrees of hawkishness among board members, making for a more nuanced, less monolithic stance within the board. Three topics should be in focus, in the following order of importance: the inflation outlook and particularly the degree of concern about services inflation; whether Banxico is at this point looking at the Fed or not; and, finally, getting a sense of how the somewhat larger fiscal deficit (already quite large at 4.9% of GDP) projected by the government this year was enough to move the risk-needle within the board when thinking about inflation risks.

Figure 1: CPI - Core goods and other services
![](images/bd521b49750db549784df10dc3f355efa33e1b9aebc7e22d492cb4cda7809000.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. J.P. Morgan forecast is labeled

Inflation data have been somewhat hard to unpack in the past couple of months, as they were subject to several atypical seasonal factors that could have distorted them, particularly in the case of services. Services ex-housing and education (“other services<sup>”</sup>) surged over 0.7%m/m, sa in March and then decelerated dramatically to just above 0.1%m/m in April, with the swing driven largely by tourism prices. The noise related to tourism seasonality should fade in May, providing a cleaner picture on the underlying momentum of services inflation. Our forecasts point to a concerning 0.57%m/m, sa gain in May. Core goods, by contrast, should still provide cover to overall core inflation (Figure 1).

An alternative way of cleansing CPI data from seasonal factors associated with Easter is looking at non-tourism services (“other services<sup>”</sup> ex. tourism prices). The message is broadly the same: underlying services inflation pressures may be building rather than waning, though we expect, based on our preliminary monthly forecast for May, the 3m/3m annualized inflation rate to ease slightly from very high levels (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Non tourism services CPI
![](images/3736f0fddf8a6d09c15c22f524ecfbc1112f79deadf39b82cba3aa5b6d0bb191.jpg)

The other two topics, the Fed and this year<sup>’</sup>s even-more-expansive fiscal policy, were mentioned either not at all in the statement (fiscal) or just fleetingly (the Fed). Discussion on the latter will likely be much richer in the minutes, and at least a few lines devoted to fiscal thrust, even if Governor Rodriguez suggested the central bank will revise down its growth forecast, which should take some of the sting out of the larger deficit in terms of demand-side inflation pressures.

On the Fed, we continue to think that given the very high real-rate domestically and the large rate differential relative to the US, there is room for Banxico to downplay its importance in the early stages of the easing cycle, but will very likely still be on the top of the board<sup>’</sup>s mind, at least given how expectations on US policy changes have impacted domestic assets performance, and particularly that of domestic rates.

## Inflation risks are to the upside

Next week<sup>’</sup>s preliminary May CPI should show headline (core) prices declining (rising) 0.18%2w/2w (+0.18%2w/2w) in the first half of the month. This would lead to a further pickup in over-year ago headline inflation to 4.82%oya from

Figure 3: GDP proxy

4.67%oya while keeping core inflation unchanged at an unrounded 4.34%oya. Mechanically, this implies that noncore inflation is the source of upside. Normally, seasonal electricity subsidies make for a very negative non-core print in 1H May, but this time around severe droughts in the country and health shocks should drive raw food prices up further. We expect rising liquefied gas prices to add insult to injury.

That said, core remains the key variable. This gauge is subject to some upside, particularly if sticky services inflation is met by a normalization in core goods inflation, which has been running low. We think this could happen against the backdrop of signs of stronger global consumer demand, particularly in the US, but much will depend of whether such strength extends over the next couple of quarters.

## Activity—Let the past be past

GDP growth was poor in the last two quarters, but more so 4Q23. The slow growth rate in 1Q, going with the flash GDP release last month was largely a result of a very weak end to 2023 and an equally disappointing start. But February data were very strong, and the flash release implicitly pointed to robust performance in March, with monthly GDP rising 1.4%m/m in February and 1.2% in March (Figure 3).

Next week we will get the final data on 1Q GDP and the March economic activity index. We look for a marginally slower 1% gain in March GDP and a 0.6% annualized gain in 1Q (from 0.8% first reported). Looking forward, carryover would be very large going into 2Q, at about 4.8%-pts, above our forecast for a 4.2%ar gain this quarter.

![](images/09f780b944a468a0e25e62414f43aa4aa5e1093cc2279e523e4aabbfa8b657ac.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. J.P. Morgan forecast for March

Of note, there were a couple of atypical seasonal factors worth monitoring, namely, leap-year and Easter holidays<sup>’</sup> timing. The first one should be less of a problem. The latter, however, can distort data significantly as seen in the largerthan-usual gaps between calendar-adjusted and non-adjusted growth rates in activity data reported so far. So it will be important to see whether momentum in April is sustained or if we witness some “statistical<sup>”</sup> payback.

The April trade data will be a good starting point next week. We look for manufacturing exports to firm 0.5% after backto-back gains in February/March. Similarly we look for small gains in non-oil consumer and intermediate goods imports; the former have been strong for many quarters, but the latter have just recently picked up. Together with firming factory exports, solid non-oil intermediate goods imports would raise our hopes for a longer-lasting lift in manufacturing than those seen in the past 1.5 years, which have proved very shallow.

## Brazil

• COPOM minutes: hawkish tone for a divided board

• We no longer expect further 50bp easing

• Monthly economic data confirm solid start of the year

• ...while ongoing floods in RS poised to hurt 2Q

The minutes of the May 8 meeting, when BCB opted to cut the policy rate by 25bp to 10.5% per year by a slim 5-4 majority, were decisively hawkish. But, even if the Board seems unanimously more hawkish than before and convinced that “the adoption of a tighter and more cautious monetary policy and with no indications about future movements was more appropriate in view of the uncertain global scenario and the domestic scenario marked by activity resilience and unanchored expectations,<sup>”</sup> the divergence among the COPOM members seems a bit wider than we initially thought. Points of disagreement go beyond the cost-benefit analysis pertaining to the specific May decision into a disagreement on the balance of risks for inflation.

This backdrop, combined with heightened uncertainty on the global scenario, justified the board<sup>’</sup>s decision to strip the forward guidance. It also makes assessing the next steps for BCB much harder, in our view, as it remains unclear what is the majority view for the June meeting and to which factors the COPOM members are more sensitive. But considering that medium-term inflation expectations appear to be on the rise, at least in the short term, we assume the easing cycle will pause at the next meeting, with the SELIC rate at 10.50%, instead of the two more 25bp cuts we had before (Figure 1). Beyond the next meeting, visibility is much more reduced, and we see that BCB will wait for more certainty on the global disinflation trend and lower domestic imbalances.

Figure 1: Nominal and real (ex-ante) SELIC rate
![](images/8b1fb44c9accacd4c134ad9dd7688b4c98172b3c4928a95a0176be32f4362287.jpg)

In the domestic arena, the board will likely gain some clarity on at least one front: activity. The next COPOM meeting will take place on June 19, while IBGE will release 1Q GDP two weeks before (June 6). The recent data seem to confirm our expectations for an upbeat start of the year (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Brazil 1Q nowcaster
![](images/cfd8b173ad19ea87164a17acc7fe4e4519a9b7139820280ef5ec884c547b7f4e.jpg)

Sentiment indicators for April continue to suggest resilience in the sector at the start of 2Q. However, there should be a significant though still unknown impact of the ongoing disaster in Brazil<sup>’</sup>s southernmost state of Rio Grande do Sul, where floods have displaced tens of thousands of people and halted economic activity in May. President Lula highlighted the federal government<sup>’</sup>s response on Wednesday, when he also created a special ministry to tackle the aid effort and further measures to support households that lost their homes due to the tragedy (Table 1).

While Congress has given its blessing for having extraordinary spending released from legal constraints, we will continue to monitor its extent, as well as its impacts on the economy, as this may influence the economic backdrop with which BCB will be faced a month from now.

%-contribution to over-year-ago core CPI

## Economic activity grew more than 4% in 1Q

Two economic indicators released over the week reinforce that activity started this year on a better note. First, the services report showed that the sector grew for the fourth of the last five months, suggesting some recovery since the weakness in late 3Q/early 4Q23. In March, service sector output was up 0.4%m/m, sa. Services output grew 2.1% in 1Q24, up from -1.4% in 4Q23, with most of the sectors showing solid growth.

Second, the IBC-Br decreased as expected but suggested that 1Q economic growth was quite solid. The IBC-Br was down 0.3%m/m, sa in March, a tenth below our call and 5bp below the consensus estimate, following a series of solid numbers. There were some small downward revisions to Jan-Feb, and upward revisions to the 2023 numbers. The IBC-Br grew 4.4%q/q, saar in 1Q, above our call of a 2.4% rise in 1Q GDP, coming from a weak +0.1% in 4Q23 (Figure 3).

![](images/1ac375c7069c8a22d0be053a7d9ec24961f2e1018e0711ca91054f34262095fa.jpg)

## Demand pressures absent from core CPI

Core CPI (ex. controlled and food CPI) stood at 2.9%oya in April, a tenth below BCB<sup>’</sup>s 3% inflation target. Our supplyrelated and demand-related core CPI estimates are running at historically low levels, with the above-average contribution coming mostly from our classification of “ambiguous<sup>”</sup> within services CPI (Figure 4).

## Argentina

Headline CPI came in at 8.8%m/m in April (289.4%oya), continuing its deceleration path

We see year-end inflation at 155%oya, on lower food inflation, postponement of regulated price adjustments

The central bank cut the monetary policy rate by 1,000bp, to 40% (3.3% monthly effective rate)

• The Omnibus Bill and the fiscal package discussion continues in the Senate commissions

Headline CPI came in at 8.8%m/m in April (289.4%oya, see details below). Inflation thus continued its deceleration path last month when compared to the 11% reported back in March and 15% average in the previous three months. Moreover, high frequency inflation data show food CPI running at 5.4%m/m through the second week of May, consistent with headline inflation level close to 5%m/m on the back of the postponement of regulated price adjustments. Against this backdrop, the central bank cut the monetary policy rate for the third time in the last month, by 1,000bp, to 40% (consistent with a monthly effective rate of 3.3%). Thus, real rates remain in negative terrain, with the government accelerating its strategy of debasing the “monetary overhang<sup>”</sup> as a prior condition for the eventual lifting of capital controls.

On the political front, the Omnibus Bill and the fiscal package discussion continues in the Senate commissions, as the government negotiates changes to the bills to secure approval. We still believe that the vote will be closer than in the Lower House, but assign a high probability of the bill being approved in the Senate. That said, the bills are likely to see modifications, requiring their return to the Lower House for ratification. The new timeline suggests the Upper House Floor will vote on the bills in the last week of May, which is why both Milei and Francos have acknowledged that the “May Pact<sup>”</sup> could be delayed into June or July.

## Down we go again

Headline CPI came in at 8.8%m/m in April (289.4%oya), in line with market expectations and above our forecast (8.2%m/ m). Inflation thus continued its deceleration path last month when compared to the 11% reported back in March and 15% average in the previous three months. This against the backdrop of the domestic demand contraction, the ongoing fiscal consolidation, and the stable 2% monthly crawlingpeg. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan<sup>’</sup>s adjustment), national headline CPI gained 8.6%m/m, sa, with the last-three-month sequential pace easing to 235.4%, saar.

Underlying CPI, both core and ex. food core, printed in the single digits for the second month in a row. Core CPI decelerated further, to 6.3%m/m (4.5%-pt contribution), from 9.4%m/m in March, and a 14% monthly average increase in the prior three months (Figure 1). Meanwhile, our preferred metric of underlying inflation (core inflation excluding food prices) also decelerated to 6%m/m. Seasonal adjustment shows ex. food core momentum strongly decelerating to 198.8% annualized, from 396.1% the previous month.

Figure 1: Headline and core National-CPI
![](images/554972e0a8a2616554de5fd9413185831dd185f7f9135c8a4539689e5c8a99a8.jpg)

Inflation deceleration remained widespread, with all categories except one easing in the month. Food prices again spearheaded the monthly deceleration, printing at 6.0%m/m (1.9%-pt contribution), compared to 10.5% (3.3%-pts) in March and a 14% average increase in the previous three months. When zooming in on food prices, the deceleration was broad-based. Meanwhile, Transportation and Education prices also decelerated strongly in the month, with their joint contribution declining by 1.3%-pts.

Regulated and seasonal prices printed again above the headline inflation level. Seasonal prices decelerated to 9.9%m/m (+1.1%-pt contribution), from a 12% average in the prior three months. Meanwhile, regulated prices printed well above headline for the fifth month in a row , at 18.4%m/m, on the back of the price adjustments in gas, electricity, and water tariffs, as well as fuels and pre-paid medical services in the month. Since December 2023, regulated prices have added 16.4%-pts to the headline print amid the ongoing relative prices correction (Figure 2).

Figure 2: National-CPI
%-pt, contribution to headline CPI
![](images/0bac384a4432144741c7a02b487dfbca495899e95547abde25ab11e919f77726.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

## Inflation to continue decelerating in May

High frequency inflation data shows food CPI running at 5.4%m/m through the second week of May. Given the announcement of the postponement of regulated price adjustments scheduled for May (see our recent note), that would be consistent with a headline inflation level close to 5%m/m. The food CPI<sup>’</sup>s weight in INDEC<sup>’</sup>s National CPI basket has increased since December 2019 by close to 5%-pts, with regulated prices<sup>’</sup> weight declining by a similar amount (-6%-pt). In turn, the strong deceleration in food CPI observed year-todate has had a stronger impact on the headline inflation level. That said, while we are still waiting for further clarity on the pace of regulated price adjustments ahead, we still estimate close to 7%-pts on aggregate in repressed inflation ahead to realign relative prices to the levels seen in 2018 (Figure 5). Note that regulated services prices are still 30% below April 2018 levels (at constant prices).

Against this backdrop, we see year-end inflation at 155%oya. Our prior base case assumed the front-loading of the bulk of the tariff adjustments in 1H24, but the above noted postponements push our 2Q24 inflation projections lower to an average of 6.5% on a monthly basis. In 2H24, inflation would average 5.5%m/m in our estimates assuming (partial) utilities<sup>’</sup> tariff adjustments resume and the government starts relaxing capital controls by 3Q23 in a gradual manner. As we<sup>’</sup>ve said, we think advancing toward promptly reducing the (still large) relative prices misalignment is a necessary condition for a sustainable disinflation path ahead.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Colombia: On a sequential basis, 1Q24 GDP posted a strong gain, up by 4.4%q/q, saar

We forecast sequential GDP to decelerate in 2Q24 (to 0.2% saar), firming up in 2H24

• 2024 annual GDP growth is fine-tuned to +1.7%y/y

• Peru: Despite March activity contraction (-0.3%m/m, sa), sequential momentum accelerated in 1Q24

We maintain our annual projection at +2.8%y/y (consistent with 3.0% 4Q/4Q)

## Colombia: Stagnant domestic demand

The national accounts report confirmed a good first quarter of the year when it comes to sequential growth, with the caveat that net exports explained the lion<sup>’</sup>s share of the dynamics, with domestic demand proving stagnant on the quarter. The quantitative details show that real GDP gained by 4.4%q/q, saar, in 1Q24, a tad above our 4.0%ar forecast. However, when taking into consideration the drastic revisions to the prior quarters, the latest performance was actually stronger than we had anticipated. Indeed, GDP growth averaged 4.2% saar in the last two quarters, which compares with just 0.4% for the whole of 2023. Thus the real GDP level for 2023 was revised higher, offering thus a higher statistical carryover than what was previously entertained. On an over-year-ago basis, GDP increased by 0.7%oya in 1Q24 (or 0.9% when controlling for seasonality and calendar effects).

As usual, we find it useful to analyze the breakdown of the demand components to illustrate the macro-dynamics at play. First and foremost, domestic demand proved stagnant in 1Q24, gaining by a whisker, 0.2%ar. When breaking down domestic demand, gross capital formation returned to the red, slipping by 2.2%ar, while consumption increased for the second consecutive quarter, by 1.3%ar. Finally, net exports have indeed explained the lion<sup>’</sup>s share of 1Q GDP performance, but on import contraction rather than exports gains. Indeed, exports (in volumes) decreased by 1.2%ar, but imports plunged by 18.7% ar (see note for further details).

Despite the series revisions, the narrative on activity remains mostly unchanged. The economy has processed the required domestic demand correction after the overheating period in 2022. But the adjustment has proved suboptimal, with investment paying the bulk of it, which puts into question potential growth rates ahead. Consumption, on the other side, has proved basically stagnant throughout the adjustment process. In all, upon revisions, in our estimates, the output gap is navigating close to home (Figure 1), but not as much in negative territory as we previously entertained (and the BanRep Staff presented in its revised central scenario, see note).

![](images/0f957e69b41e541f04140ee53aa423ccec2cbf01e45a68f342b187b1323b5999.jpg)

When it comes to forecasts, following the revisions to prior quarters and the 1Q24 print, we project sequential GDP to decelerate in 2Q24 (to 0.2%ar), firming up in 2H24 (averaging 2.5%ar). Annual GDP growth is fine-tuned to +1.7%y/ y, consistent with 2.4%4Q/4Q.

## Peru: Overall good 1Q24 activity

When adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects, the activity index came in at -0.3 %m/m, sa, on the heels of a 1.1% gain in February. The sequential momentum accelerated to 4.1%3m/3m, saar from the 2.1% pace observed in 4Q23. On an over-year-ago basis, calendar days proved a drag, as two fewer working days drove the annual variation to record in the red, at -0.3%oya (Figure 2). Yet, pending revisions, when activity is tracked with the series adjusted for seasonal and calendar effects, the annual change posts a 1.9%oya gain.

Figure 2: Peru real activity
![](images/457b9e786c53f58ed5bf54d02209f1453327ebb63e7805ef47ccefa2834480fa.jpg)

While waiting for the quarterly national accounts, the monthly report came in pretty much aligned with our seasonally adjusted forecast (4.3%ar for 1Q24), confirming the good above-potential sequential performance in the first quarter of the year, though leaving a negative carryover for the second quarter. We thus maintain the view that the mediocre performance observed in the post-Covid recovery is in the rear-view mirror, though by Mar<sup>’</sup>24 headline activity printed only 4.0 % above pre-pandemic levels as of February 2020 (Figure 2).

Ahead, monetary easing should allow some sectors to regain and sustain positive momentum, such as construction and some services. Also, El Niño proved very moderate, and terms of trade is proving more supportive than expected. Pending the quarterly national accounts reports, we maintain our view for 2Q24 GDP to moderate to +2.8%q/q, ar, as well as the annual projection at +2.8%y/y (consistent with 3.0% 4Q/4Q).

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

• Weak employment prompts another rise in the unemployment rate

• Private pay growth accelerated to 5.2% 3m/3m, saar

• We sticking with our August cut forecast for now

This week the labour market report was mixed, with evidence of weak job demand alongside reaccelerating wage costs. We note some tentative evidence that the GDP upswing this year is putting resources under strain, as the workweek has shot higher with no signs of better productivity. That assumes the hours data are sending an accurate signal, however. Next week the main focus will be on inflation. Headline is set to drop back to the target and core will fall too. But services prices are under scrutiny. We forecast a slightly softer outturn than forecast by the BoE, but see upside risk because many firms reset their prices in April. We see a close call for a BoE cut between June and August, and will review that forecast after the CPI report.

Megan Greene this week gave a more balance speech focussed on labour hoarding. While there are two sided risks to that phenomenon, the downside scenario of job shedding appears unlikely when GDP is accelerating as it has been. She noted that “the burden of proof [lies] in inflation persistence continuing to wane<sup>”</sup>. This seems to suggest she is not on board to vote for a cut yet, absent further evidence of cooling wage and services inflation pressures. With pay growth remaining high and limited signs of cooling this week, this also places a lot of emphasis on next week<sup>’</sup>s data (April pay settlements as well as the CPI next Wednesday). Bailey is also scheduled to speak next Tuesday at 6pm BST.

Figure 1: ILO unemployment rate
![](images/cd0775cbb4dcaeb3ab694cea723f0ae6833cc89a9dd26d9d235ee41c77d51dd0.jpg)

## Jobs market weak, pay growth running hot

Caution is still required when interpreting the labour market data at the moment, but the ILO unemployment rate derived from the LFS household survey moved up again from 4.2% to

4.3% in March (Figure 1). It now stands at levels seen last summer, although is in line with the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecast. Employment growth from the same survey remained negative with the annualized rate down 2.1% on a 3m/3m basis. Labour supply was flat to leave the participation rate at 62.6%.

Other data might help to corroborate the message from the LFS survey. The claimant count unemployment rate was revised up to 4.1% but shows a flat trajectory since last October. The payroll based jobs data fell 0.3%m/m in April (or 85k). But the decline reported in the prior month was almost entirely revised away, showing that even these data have caveats. Payroll job growth has decelerated of late and is now running close to flat. Some of the jobs surveys are mixed, but overall point to employment that is at least stabilizing. Meanwhile, vacancies continued to fall but the vacancy rate has recently stabilized above its pre-pandemic level.

Private sector average weekly earnings rose 0.6%m/m in March, taking the 3m/3m annualized rate back up to 5.2% (Figure 2). The data are volatile but this is a second consecutive upside surprise. The three month smoothed year ago rate fell from 6.0% to 5.9%. Although this was above the consensus, it is a small downside compared to the 6.0% forecast published by the BoE for 1Q last week.

Figure 2: Average weekly earnings: private sector
%, excl. bonuses, all rates of change are annualized and seasonally adj.
![](images/a8b9c195e5227c1a2c8ffc4d1ccbe64199e23b1f1b2a83c8167b0a0601b7de8b.jpg)

There had been some confusion about what the BoE<sup>’</sup>s forecast actually was for this release. The Bank published a spreadsheet straight after its decision last week, which in one location showed a 5.6% forecast for 1Q, hence implying a -0.3%m/m in March (assuming no revisions). That was subsequently edited to show the 6.0% mentioned above. In the event, the data didn<sup>’</sup>t deliver on those lofty expectations for 1Q. But if pay continues to run at this pace then it would comfortably exceed the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 2Q forecast for a slowing in the year ago rate to 5.1%

Next month the April data will include the impact of the rise in the National Living Wage. There was some suggestion of this being implemented early by firms, which could potentially explain some of the strength in the March data. But there was also a big jump in the PMI input prices reading in April, while the timely payroll based wage data in this release showed a strong 0.9%m/m rise in the median whole economy figures for the same month. That is above our current forecast for the April AWE, and implies no payback from the 0.6% outturn reported in March.

The caveat is that these payroll based data include bonuses. However, a private sector proxy we have replicated from the data show monthly pay gains stepping up from 0.5% in March to 1.1% in April. If the AWE private sector average earnings numbers come in anywhere close to these figures in April, it would leave pay on track to come in at least a couple of tenths stronger than the BoE<sup>’</sup>s 2Q forecast.

We see elevated risks of a June cut from the BoE but believe the odds are close to even rather than a foregone conclusion. The data in this report are mixed, notwithstanding the point on pay mentioned above. Growth is recovering more strongly than the BoE expects. There is no clear sign as yet that this is generating more hiring, but average hours worked spiked in 1Q leaving total hours up 3.8% annualized (Figure 3). This suggests productivity declined again in 1Q despite GDP growth having recovered. The question is whether the sharp rise in the workweek in 1Q - if the data can be trusted - is signalling that renewed internal labour demand is putting more pressure on pay outturns.

Figure 3: Average hours worked per week
![](images/b69ba522d9030d33fa084a314ae92de11be654172c40cc60b8f693ad085035b3.jpg)
We have left our forecast for the first BoE cut unchanged at August, but that is looking very close and we will review it again after seeing next week<sup>’</sup>s April data for inflation and pay settlements.

A large 12% drop in regulated domestic energy bills is set to pull down significantly on headline inflation in April, offsetting an expected rise in liquid fuel and alcohol and tobacco prices. Core inflation too is likely to step down, as the outsized gains seen last spring appear unlikely to be repeated. We think core goods prices will be broadly flat over the month in seasonally adjusted terms, while we have a 0.4%m/ m, sa gain in services. This would take year ago services inflation down from 6.0% to 5.4%, a touch below the BoE<sup>’</sup>s recently revised forecast of 5.5% for the month. April is a special month, however, many services firms reset their prices infrequently and often at the start of the UK financial year. This means scope for bigger surprises in April, given the large swings in business input costs (energy falling, wages rising). Arguably there is some upside risk around this number.

## Sweden and Norway

Riksbank cut in May, but closed the door for June

• Soft momentum in Scandi inflation reports

• Modest growth in Norway<sup>’</sup>s divided economy

The Riksbank decided to lower the policy rate to 3.75% at the May meeting. The cut was justified by a broad set of domestic data, including the March inflation report, a fall in the NIER survey price indicators, and a rise in the unemployment rate. The Board did, however, also mention some hawkish (external) factors such as the US economy, higher rates abroad, and the krona. Consequently, the door was closed to another cut in June. Instead, the rate path from the March meeting was validated, which showed two more cuts in 2H24.

The minutes largely followed the tone from the press conference. While members are more positive about inflation prospects, they remained concerned about external factors. Uncertainty was also expressed about how policy rate cuts would affect activity and inflation given Sweden<sup>’</sup>s high interest rate sensitivity. Therefore, although the domestic picture could justify launching a series of rapid rate cuts, gradualism was favoured. We still expect a next cut in September, with an elevated risk of a cut already in August.

The risk of an early move is linked to the April CPI report, which again surprised to the downside. Core prices rose 0.4%m/m with the year-ago rate unchanged at 2.9%oya, a notch below our call and well-below the Riksbank forecast. Momentum-wise core inflation fell 0.3%-pt to 0.9%3m, saar. The report suggests that the very soft print in March was not just a one-off, validating the Riksbank<sup>’</sup>s decision to cut rates in May. There were, however, a few caveats with e.g. a puzzling sharp fall in the volatile package holidays, in turn creating upside risk for the next print in May. Here, services inflation also faces upside pressure due to Sweden hosting Eurovision and three Taylor Swift concerts. Last year, a Beyonce concert contributed 0.2%-pt to inflation rate.

Swedish services PMI plummeted 6pts to 48.1 in April, leading to the composite index dropping 4pts to 49.0. This is consistent with GDP contracting 0.6%q/q, saar. The message of contracting growth was clearly conflicting with the NIER survey (+0.6%), our 2Q forecast (+1.0%), and the Riksbank<sup>’</sup>s forecast (+1.5%). In the press release, the downturn in April was described as surprising and “perhaps temporary<sup>”</sup>. We lean towards this interpretation as well given the inconsistency with other activity indicators (Figure 1). We will receive more information in the next couple of weeks with the NIER survey for May where we look for a further pickup in the headline index. We also expect a small upward revision to the 1Q GDP number from -0.4% to -0.1%q/q, saar.

## Norway: Divided economy

Norway<sup>’</sup>s core prices increased 0.9%m/m, nsa, in April, leading to a 0.1%-pt drop in the year-ago rate to 4.4%oya, above expectations (except for Norges Bank). Momentum wise, core inflation dropped 0.4%-pt to 2.8% on 3m/3m% basis. The upward surprise was driven by food prices which contributed 0.44%-pts to the monthly rate. This is, however, likely to be a temporary driver judging by leading indicators. Outside of food, the price pressure was modest in April.

The Revised Budget hinted at limited changes to Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s FX purchases. NOK sales should average NOK 0.45-0.5bn/day in 2024, implying a continuation of the current pace 0.55bn/day (and hence also our forecast for June). The Budget showed a +0.7%-pt change in the structural nonoil deficit, indicating highly expansionary fiscal policy.

Mainland GDP grew 0.6%q/q, saar in 1Q24. Details showed a divided economy with private domestic final sales contracting 8.4%q/q, saar, whilst government consumption and net exports once again made sold contributions (Figure 2). Labour market numbers increased more than GDP, implying another fall in productivity. Despite the core part of the economy clearly being pressured by high interest rates, we think Norges Bank will focus more on the headline GDP number and labor market data beating expectations. Hence, the report should be hawkish for Norges Bank.

## Emerging Europe

• Israel: Inflation up again, 1Q GDP a tad softer than expected

Romania: soft growth in 1Q24, but rebound likely in 2Q

Romania: NBR stayed on hold at 7%, revised up the inflation forecast and took a hawkish stance

• Czech Republic: beyond the noise, core CPI remains resilient

## Israel: Inflation upside, GDP downside

Two upside inflation surprises in a row are, perhaps, not enough to call a trend, but do look somewhat worrying. In March, tobacco prices were to blame, although there were already signs of a more broad-based pick-up in momentum. In April, airfares and housing added to inflation considerably, even as median momentum moderated slightly thanks to softer dynamics in food and non-food goods. There is a good chance that the spike in airfares will partially reverse in May. However, the rise in rents appears more fundamental to us. Although the catch up to rising housing prices began slightly earlier than we had anticipated, it will probably continue in coming months, especially as constrained supply should continue to support prices. Besides, continued economic recovery could further strengthen the underlying inflation trend in coming quarters. We expect inflation to end this year at about 2.6%oya (Figure 1, see more detail here).

Figure 1: Israel consumer inflation
![](images/735eec24f448c057affc4c5c63396e9b78d41b87158e51847a4bf00cb0c4b9fb.jpg)

The first estimate of 1Q GDP printed at 14.1%q/q, saar, below expectations (JPMe 15.5, consensus 14.7), while 4Q was revised slightly lower to -21.7%. Given the scale of recent swings in activity, we are tempted to call this miss modest. All domestic demand components posted very strong gains last quarter. Yet, with the exception of public consumption, levels remain visibly below pre-war marks (Table 1). The main surprises to us were (i) a substantial decline of 11.0% ar in exports in 1Q, which contradicts the strength in reported monthly exports of both goods and services, and (ii) only a moderate recovery in residential investment (construction) activity last quarter, with activity staying 35% below pre-war levels. We anticipate the pace of GDP recovery to moderate to about 7% ar in 2Q and 6% in 3Q, with GDP returning to pre-war levels by 3Q. Our forecast for full-year GDP growth of 1.8% remains unchanged.

Source: CBS, J.P. Morgan

For the BoI, we suspect the recent inflation surprises will outweigh the GDP news. Inflation is running above the targeted 2% despite the large negative GDP shock, which won<sup>’</sup>t add to policymakers<sup>’</sup> comfort. The expected VAT hike has already added to near-term inflation expectations and the BoI will probably want to track second-order effects of tax decisions in the coming quarters. Most importantly, the geopolitical environment remains tense and Israel<sup>’</sup>s risk premia appear to have edged higher recently. In these circumstances, we think it will be hard for the BoI to continue to ease policy in the near term. We no longer expect a cut at the May meeting. We keep 50bp in cuts in 2H24, but the delivery will depend on easing of geopolitical risks as well as more supportive global markets environment (the Fed cutting).

## Romania: soft growth in 1Q24

Flash 1Q24 GDP was up only 0.5%q/q sa, significantly below the 1.2% JPM forecast or 0.9% of consensus; it is a soft reading also because 4Q23 posted a large contraction of -0.6%q/q (revised down from -0.4%). No details are given in the flash release, but retail sales were very strong (5%q/q, sa and not annualized), IP was weak (and contracted about 1%q/q); construction it seems contracted sharply (-25.9% q/q not annualized on Jan-Feb data). It could be that the large contraction in construction points to investment contraction and explains weaker growth recovery relative to the forecast. On overyear-ago basis, growth was 1.7%oya relative to 3.3% expected but still marks an improvement from 1.1% in 4Q23.

GDP growth is likely on an uptrend over rest of 2024, but we revise full 2024E to 3.1% from 3.5% previously. If we are right that limited investment acted as a drag in 1Q24, we

should see a significant rebound in 2Q24 (we have increased the forecast for the sequential pace to 6.1% saar from 4.1% and have pushed higher 2H24 too). As oya growth has moved higher in 1Q24, we expect that to continue and see 4Q24 at about 5%, bringing full 2024 GDP growth to 3.1% (from 3.5% in our prior forecast); we made no changes to GDP growth in 2025E, still at 3.5%. Despite the soft start, we see upside risks to GDP growth in 2024 on elections and EUfunded projects.

The soft GDP print carries limited implications for both inflation and the central bank. Based on retail sales (Figure 2), the consumer is much more robust than reflected in the 1Q GDP release. In addition, we believe GDP growth is likely to accelerate. Even if we assume that GDP growth is weak and Romania is already facing a negative output gap, the inflationary implications from fiscal policy come to the forefront. The government has not adjusted the budget balance over 2022-2023 and the risk is for 2024 to post as high or higher budget deficit. There are also higher wages in the public sector and pensions are scheduled to increase starting September. Fiscal policy represents a clear upside risk to the inflation outlook and has been highlighted repeatedly by the NBR, and will limit the amount of monetary easing.

Figure 2: Retail sales, Romania vs CE3
![](images/bfad4819e45443a2fffb3e4ebe828841eb10028d78ded2e34e1e32e33e56b64c.jpg)

## Romania: at best two rate cuts in 2024

National Bank of Romania (NBR) kept the key rate unchanged at 7% in a rate decision which we saw as a coin toss (see here). Other policy parameters were left unchanged too. The statement was on the hawkish side.The NBR now sees a higher inflation path in the short term and has also delayed the forecast fall inside the target band (1.5% to 3.5%) to March 2026 from end-2025. The statement also points to commodities being a less powerful disinflationary force than previously assumed, especially over short-term.

The new NBR inflation forecast (published on Wednesday) includes some changes in the case of core inflation, while those for headline are small. Core is now 0.3%-pts higher at end-24 (5.3%oya vs 6% in our forecast) and it is 0.1%-pts higher at end-25 (3.7%oya compared to 4.3% in our forecast)

\- Figure 3. The higher core forecast is not driven by a positive output gap as the latter was revised lower by the NBR over 4Q23-1Q25 (this implies increased non-demand inflation per sistence). For headline, end-24 was revised higher 0.2%-pts (to 4.9% vs. 5% in our forecast) while end-25 was kept unchanged at 3.5%oya compared to 4.7% in our forecast (Figure 4)- we see this as optimistic but NBR will likely adjust higher gradually. We forecast more core persistence and growth recovery which keeps inflationary pressures strong, especially in services. We think risks to our 2H25 forecast are to the upside from possible VAT hike as a new government aims to consolidate fiscally.

NBR probably turned more cautious on a combination of factors, not just the inflation outlook. As revisions to the inflation forecast are rather small, even for core, it is tough to describe them as main driver for the decision on policy rates this month. It is possible that the NBR Board expected inflation to decline faster than forecasted for 1Q24. It is also possible that worse fiscal execution during 1Q24 played a role in removing the desire to cut rates in April/May. Global developments might have had an impact too, but recall that NBR Board shifted cautious already at the early April rate meeting, ahead of the market re-pricing of the Fed.

The new inflation forecast and Governor statements are consistent with rate cuts this year. Governor Isarescu dismissed expectations of a rate cut in May by arguing that he indicated that “at least<sup>”</sup> two months of disinflation are needed - this implies the bias remains to cut rates. But timing remains unclear amid limited space, as both inflation and fiscal risks could force rates unchanged all year. The Governor stated that he cannot say how much or when is the NBR likely to cut, but underscored that the central bank is “data-dependent<sup>”</sup> and still ponders the right timing for rate cuts. He then clarified that NBR does not provide forward guidance and is always datadependent. He described rate cuts in July or August as not “certain<sup>”</sup> , but also did not dismiss either month as possible. We think the more relevant message from the Governor is that ability to cut rates is unclear for the whole 2024 - an indication that while there exists a bias to cut rates, it might not be possible. He was clear that NBR has no choice but to keep monetary policy tight while it awaits and encourages the government to implement the fiscal correction which might start only in early 2025, after general elections in December.

Our view is that first 25bp rate cut will be delivered in August and see 50bp in rate cuts this year, but risks for no cuts. Since late 2023 we had two rate cuts for 2024, in August and November. We added a cut in May after the February rate meeting delivered unexpected and uncharacteristic forward guidance. While we recognise the possibility of a rate cut in July, we doubt that NBR will be able to deliver more than

50bp in cuts this year. August and November might not be the months when NBR cuts, but it is hard to see a sharp fall in inflation that would outweigh fiscal risks and would give the NBR comfort to cut rates by more than 50bp this year. The main risk to our view is that NBR does not cut at all in 2024 or cuts only once.

## Czech Republic: resilient core CPI

After a noisy start of the year, with a very subdued (tax contaminated) core inflation print in January, followed by two stronger readings in February/March, April data added to the confusion. At first sight, we have an unexpectedly high inflation print, with CPI accelerating to 2.9%oya (Figure 5), from 2%, much more than expected by us, the market and the CNB (Consensus: 2.4%oya, JPM 2.5%, CNB: 2.5%).

Figure 5: Czech headline CPI
![](images/adb33cae5819c87907a63eba2b0332f77bbf0f6f8783bd6c57eb3d8ca09c4c71.jpg)

Looking in the details however, we conclude this is led by non-core items whereas core inflation is actually benign vs expectations. Breaking the recent spell of weakness, food prices jumped 1.7%m/m (0% expected) driven by a wide range of subcomponents, while alcohol and tobacco prices jumped 2.2%. Core inflation as measured by the CNB (inflation excluding food, alcohol, tobacco, energy, regulated items and adjusted for first round impact of indirect tax changes) was up just 0.1%m/m nsa (much lower than our expectation of 0.4%), leading to a decline of the over-year-ago rate to 2.6%oya (from 2.7% in March), compared to the 2.9% we had expected. This would be great news.

Figure 6: Czech core CPI
![](images/e038f0ff448c6dab045c129e0bc90c594d19a39f6769795d6941bd262b3c8850.jpg)

Figure 7: Core CPI sequential pace %mm saar
![](images/3ae314f3ca785de79fbc7f0fcef7043a4e2e1ce873c14a45601120f80ad101d6.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan estimates

Further analysis however, leads us to conclude that were it not for heightened volatility in package holidays, core would be stuck above target (closer to 3%). Does this mean April was bad? Not in relative terms, as it was actually marginally better than February and March. But in absolute terms this is still showing inflation that is too high. Looking within core CPI, goods momentum strengthened further in April to around 0.25%m/m sa or 3% in annualized terms. Goods are the part of the basket which has helped keep core inflation low, masking the strength in services, but now even goods are strong. Core services are even more contaminated by package holidays than broader core, and so here it makes sense also to look at the indicator excluding that item. Service ex. rents and holidays slowed a bit versus March, but still printed high at 0.3%m/m sa, around 3.75% at annualized pace.

![](images/cda50e04af147adf5e5ce30aa3e75305c1a50aaed7ad8e96af97cb286cbed4df.jpg)

Week of May 20- 24

Hungary:
<table><tr><td>Tue</td><td>NBH rate decision</td></tr><tr><td>May 21</td><td>50bp cut to 7.25%</td></tr></table>

We expect the NBH to cut interest rates by another 50bps to 7.25%. The latest inflation prints have not been great, with core CPI and services in particular surprising significantly to the upside, and the data suggest increased backward looking price indexation. However, the NBH does not appear too worried, with Deputy Governor Virag this week reaffirming guidance of rates at 6.75-7% by the end of June, which makes a 50bp cut next week nearly inevitable. The strength in the Forint recently together with a more favorable risk environment increase room for maneuver.

We expect the CBRT to keep its policy rate on hold at 50% with hawkish bias in its forward guidance. The CBRT will likely be on hold at 50% until 4Q24 to monitor the impact of tightening measures on still strong domestic demand and the underlying trend of monthly inflation. The CBRT might hint macroprudential measures in its MPC statement to curb FX loan growth which accelerated in recent months.

## Industrial output

## South Africa

• High frequency data points to 1Q activity underperformance

We trim 1Q GDP forecast to 0.15%q/q (0.6% saar), from 0.4% (1.8% saar)

• Upward 2Q growth revision lowers impact on full year GDP forecast, now at 1% for 2024

Inflation likely was stable 5.3%oya in April, with core dropping to 4.6%

High frequency activity data released this week confirmed a weak IP performance in 1Q (Figure 1). We flagged the downside GDP growth bias last week after a March decline in manufacturing output to track a 0.9%q/q as (or -3.7% ar) contraction in 1Q. Mining output followed suit this week with a 5%m/m sa fall, and we now see a 1.7%q/q drop (-6.5% ar) for last quarter. As a result, we estimate IP to have shaved 0.8%pt from GDP last quarter as logistics hurdles and power shortages weighed on the energy intensive sectors.As a result, we lower our 1Q GDP growth estimate to +0.15%q/q sa (0.6% ar) from 0.4% (1.8% ar).

Figure 1: South Africa IP production
![](images/53aacdb997a1ea5e936dfd24c5bb3985b2be0601d14990c0b2b979fd6bb914f6.jpg)

The growth revisions embed likely subdued household consumption last quarter. Retail sales growth accelerated to 1.4%m/m in March, with the increase probably related to Easter holiday shopping. Yet, the gains come after negative prints in January and February, resulting in a 0.9%q/q contraction in 1Q24, following no change in Q423. At the same time, vehicle sales and wholesale trade (also heaviest-weighted consumption components) contracted last quarter. Gains in trade and financial services activity and in agriculture should buoy overall growth last quarter, failure of which could drive a small GDP contraction.

Our full year 2024 GDP growth estimate is two-tenths lower at 1%y/y. As we lower the forecast for 1Q we simultaneously raise 2Q GDP growth to 0.6%q/q (2.5%q/q saar), from 0.4%, an optimism buoyed reduced power outages. The energy availability factor continued to improve, climbing to 65% of full capacity as of May 11, from 48% at the start of April (Figure 2). As a result, the country has experienced no power cuts since April, which should support IP in 2Q. In addition, retail sales probably recovers in 2Q as general dealers (the largest category and energy-intensive users) benefit from the stable energy supply.

Figure 2: Weekly energy availability factor (EAF)
![](images/a889b22c3a87786a8774d3691cf69e5a2fd6b869183f39647ea57d76a7e1196c.jpg)

## Inflation likely stable in April

The upcoming April inflation report will likely show headline inflation unchanged at 5.3%oya despite higher transportation costs (Figure 3). Transport costs probably rose to 6.2%oya (from 5.3%), largely on price hikes at the pump for petrol (65c/l) and diesel (3.2c/l) in April. Yet, food inflation likely eased further to 4.6%oya, from 4.9% in March, largely on base effects as prices were up 0.4%m/m (from -0.1%). Our projection incorporates a moderate impact of El Niño, so we think food inflation bottomed in April, and should begin to climb to about 6.5% early next year. Meanwhile, the impact of the front-loading of medical insurance likely faded in April, pulling core inflation sharply lower to 4.6%oya (from 4.9%).

Figure 3: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/3fd1b84400fe482d87766b379f41fda631a631293dfbb45a7870404e9c28e6d6.jpg)
Source: StatsSA, J.P. Morgan forecast
