## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia<sup>’</sup>s FY25 budget reported an expected surplus of A\$9.3b for FY24, close to expectations

• But fiscal policy was eased more than expected in FY25 and beyond

• Australian 1Q24 wage growth undershot expectations and the April unemployment rate overshot

• We expect the RBNZ to remain on hold in the May meeting

Australia<sup>’</sup>s FY25 Commonwealth Budget should consolidate the perception of a balanced policy outlook, where full return to the inflation target won<sup>’</sup>t look assured for some time.

Treasury has projected an underlying cash balance for FY24 as a surplus A\$9.3bn, close to our forecast of A\$8bn. This is materially better than the Treasury expected back at MYEFO, but weaker parameter assumptions, cost of living relief and other discretionary fiscal measures are assumed to erode all of that over FY25 and F26. The FY25 deficit is projected to be -A\$28.3bn (-1% of GDP), nearly A\$10bn worse than expected at MYEFO, and A\$15bn worse on net through the forward estimates.

There will be plenty of debate about whether the newer household support measures will create monetary policy externalities by adding to demand. While the energy rebates are straightforwardly negative for inflation, the overall fiscal impulse due to other spending channels is larger - in total, we estimate an extra 0.3-0.4%-pt on GDP growth.

Conventional multipliers suggest this won<sup>’</sup>t be enough to change the medium-term inflation trajectory a lot, but the RBA board is already “uncertain<sup>”</sup> about when it will achieve the inflation target; the outlook is now cloudier still. The subsidy effect could mechanically lower inflation to the low 3s this year, but getting to around 2.5% soon looks less assured. We still assume the RBA will be on hold until 1Q25.

## Wage growth peaked

Australian nominal wages rose 0.8%q/q in 1Q24, below J.P. Morgan and consensus expectations (+0.9%q/q), but with a one-tenth upward revision to 4Q (+1%q/q). The outcome sees the annual rate decrease 0.1%-pt to 4.1%oya, consistent with our view that 4Q23 was the high water mark for annual wage growth this cycle. In the details, private sector wages grew 0.8%q/q, while public sector wages rose 0.5%q/q.

By industry, wage growth was strongest amongst health care (5.3%oya), and utilities and retail trade (4.4%oya). Financial services (3.6%oya) and wholesale trade (3.4%oya) remained notable laggards. Some variation across sectors reflects the slow-moving nature of collective agreements, which are now reflecting earlier tightening in the labor market (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Wage growth vs. share of individual agreements
![](images/301058f4606e22256f66de3ccf5ca47deaf59053166720e250a87e1db57b7aa3.jpg)

This week<sup>’</sup>s print has a relatively limited read-through for the inflation outlook. Labor cost growth tends to lag inflation, probably because prices are updated more frequently than wages, so isn<sup>’</sup>t much help for forecasting inflation. The flipside is that continued moderation in inflation would provide comfort that wage/inflation psychology has not changed, and wages are on track to settle at target consistent levels.

## Unemployment deja vu

Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate increased 0.2%-pts to 4.1% in April and the March figure was also revised a tenth higher to 3.9%. The result was above both J.P. Morgan and consensus expectations (3.9%), although the ABS<sup>’</sup> guidance suggests some of the increase appears to be due to the same seasonality dynamics which plagued the data at the turn of the year.

Figure 2: Underemployment and unemployment rates % of labour force, both axes
![](images/32176eacd3f55bc5cd9cb6fe472cac73479ecfc8de18a879a030717d1d153c5f.jpg)

In the media release, the ABS notes that part of the rise in unemployed reflected “more people than usual indicating that they had a job they were waiting to start in.<sup>”</sup> The rise in unemployment in January was, similarly, partly due to a rise in the number of people reporting they expected to commence work, and the increase was subsequently unwound in February as those individuals commenced jobs (Figure 2). Holiday effects in the first month of each quarter were similarly noted as being more prominent lately.

We might expect some payback in the May numbers based on this dynamic, although the ABS also notes that the increase in marginally attached workers only accounted for some of the rise in the April data. Our conclusion is that a jobless rate somewhere in the high 3s-4% is about right, but seasonality issues have made it hard to glean much from the month to month changes about the direction of travel.

## RBA minutes to reinforce rates risk

In the RBA<sup>’</sup>s May meeting, the Board maintained the guidance that the “path of interest rates that will best ensure inflations returns to target in a reasonable time-frame remains uncertain and the Board is not ruling anything in or out.<sup>”</sup> The concluding paragraph contained a sense of growing impatience, as the board is now “vigilant to upside risks.<sup>”</sup> We expect the minutes to also convey that the risks to the inflation/rates outlook have shifted to the upside.

Prior to March, the Board had used the minutes to communicate that, “members considered whether to raise the cash rate target by a further 25 basis points at this meeting or to leave it unchanged.<sup>”</sup> In May<sup>’</sup>s press conference, Governor Bullock noted the Board had discussed both the option of remaining on hold or hiking. Reinserting the commentary in the minutes would reinforce risks were skewed towards further tightening after the stronger 1Q inflation data.

The Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecasts were also refreshed in May, with upgrades to inflation and labor market outlook. While the near-term upward revisions to inflation reflect higher fuel prices, we expect most of the commentary on the inflation outlook to centre upon the stickiness of services inflation. The Board will likely also link the stronger than expected labor market outcomes to the persistence of services inflation and reiterate that the labor market remains tighter than consistent with low and stable inflation.

## RBA minutes to reinforce rates risk

The RBNZ meets next week and will release updated MPS projections. We forecast the cash rate to remain steady and don<sup>’</sup>t expect much change in the inflation forecasts, which already show achievement of the target band in 2H24. That has looked assured on the trajectory of recent data as core has slowed a lot, and only base effects are keeping the annual rate up until 3Q. The committee has been stating its confidence that policy is restrictive and the target band will be achieved soon. This week<sup>’</sup>s inflation expectations offer corroboration as the 2Y metric moved further inside the target band, close to the midpoint. That this has occurred against firming fuel prices suggests domestic pricing behaviour is responding to weak activity outcomes and the return of excess capacity.

That said, the committee has also wanted to convey a degree of pain tolerance, by only forecasting rate cuts to occur around mid-25. A case could easily be made for bringing cuts forward in the staff<sup>’</sup>s OCR track, but we have not expected the RBNZ to pre-emptively signal cuts, instead preferring to pivot suddenly once they are convinced the target is achieved. A hint that this is coming should be most evident in the discussion on growth, the labor market and housing, which continue to deteriorate. With inbound immigration having slowed lately, it is also possible that growth is downgraded, but the message is never straightforward and often comes with the confusing qualifier that potential growth may also be lower.

## Greater China

• China: Domestic demand softened in April

CPI inflation rate ticked up modestly; PPI deflation lingered

• Credit report came as a big disappointment

• Accelerated home price decline in April

• U.S. announced tariff increases on targeted Chinese goods

The April activity data suggest a further slowing in domestic demand. Retail sales and fixed investment both came in below expectations in April, with IP growth also below our forecasts, raising concerns that the economy has lost momentum after the solid 1Q recovery, as happened this time last year.

The weak activity in April is in part due to a fading LNY effect, but is also driven by policy reversal. In particular, the stronger-than-expected 1Q GDP report may have led to a sense of complacency among policymakers and a dial-back of policy support. For instance, April credit growth (TSF) hit a new record low, at 8.3%oya, and TSF flow registered the first decline since 2005. But policy attitudes also seem to have changed. The government bond component in TSF declined by 98bn yuan in April, the largest monthly decline on record. On the monetary policy front, the PBOC Monetary Policy Report 1Q24 stated that credit deceleration is normal amid structural transformation, and the quality of credit growth matters more than the quantity of credit growth. Such a defensive, or hawkish, attitude contributed to a policy reversal and activity slowdown in April.

While the policy reversal in April is concerning, the good news is that the Politburo meeting in late April seems to have noticed the problem, and emphasized the importance of policy continuity. In particular, the Politburo called for early issuance of ultra-long central government bonds (1 trillion yuan this year) and accelerated issuance of special local government bonds (3.9 trillion yuan this year). It also called for flexible use of interest rate and RRR policy instrument and a lower funding cost for the real economy.

An encouraging change is that the pace of government bond issuance has picked up after the Politburo meeting. In the first 17 days of May, the net increase in government bond issuance registered nearly 600 billion yuan, compared to the 98 billion yuan contraction in April. On 17 May, the first bond was issued under the 1 trillion yuan ultra-long special central government bond program. Given that the pace of government bond issuance was relatively slow in the first four months of the year, we think the utilization of remaining fiscal space

will provide support for growth in 2H24.

On the monetary policy front, we maintain our baseline forecast for the next policy rate cut in June and a 25bp RRR cut in 2H. We expect TSF growth to pick up modestly to 9.0% toward year-end.

Another interesting development is the accelerated pace of housing policy relaxation amid the continuous weakness in housing market activity. The PBOC announced three measures on 17 May: (1) a reduction of the mortgage rate from Home Provident Fund by 25bps; (2) a lowering of the minimum downpayment requirement to 15% for first-home mortgages and 25% for second-home mortgages; and (3) a removal of the floor restrictions for first- and second-home mortgage interest rates. In addition, a new policy initiative for the government to buy unsold homes has been launched, which is a desirable policy move to reduce housing inventory, increase public housing investment and indirectly encourage consumption from beneficiary households. Whether this new policy initiative will change housing market dynamics depends on the scale of the program and the funding support. As local governments are generally facing fiscal difficulties, near-term options may have to come from the central bank (via PSL) or the utilization of special local government bond proceeds, if it is meant to make a meaningful impact.

## Domestic demand conditions softened

April IP rose 6.7%oya (Figure 1), compared to 4.5%oya in March. On our estimates, IP fell 0.5% m/m sa in April, adding to the decline of 1.0% m/m sa in March. The details suggest modest growth in electricity production in April (3.1%oya), solid growth in electric machinery equipment production (5.8%oya), significant growth in auto production (16.3%oya), as well as the production of communication, computer and other electronic equipment (15.6%oya). Production activity in construction-related sectors continued to underperform, including a decline in the output of crude steel and cement.

Figure 1: IP growth and leading sub-sectors
![](images/42248439dc597eeec94204d685cf7dbafee5d16b8a963877365d1efd79dbc12b.jpg)
Source: NBS

Fixed investment came in below expectations, growing 4.2% oya for Jan-Apr and 3.6%oya in April (Figure 2). The details suggest continuous contraction in real estate FAI, with outperformance in manufacturing FAI, along with moderate easing in infrastructure FAI growth. By ownership breakdown, private investment showed modest growth, at 0.3%oya in Jan-Apr (vs. 0.5%oya in Jan-Mar), while public investment rose 7.4%oya (vs. 7.8%oya for Jan-Feb).

2013 2014 2015 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan
Figure 2: China nominal fixed asset investment growth
![](images/e96b9b6f8c310e39d897c062ebe6083e73931eefaea5f4fe40675b96874c7371.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

Housing market activity stayed weak in April. New home sales (area) fell 17.1%oya in April (vs. -27.1%oya in March), and new home sale (value) fell 29.2%oya (vs. -31.2%oya in March). New home starts (floor space) fell 13.7%oya in April (vs. -26.7%oya in March). Meanwhile, following strong growth last year, new home completion (area) has slowed recently, falling 13.4%oya in April (vs. -25.1%oya in March).

Figure 3: China retail sales
![](images/2dc785fac7054d962c54409758d173751b3e37547692bcb5b8c7c17a01d83bd7.jpg)

Nominal retail sales came in below expectations, growing 2.3%oya in April (Figure 3), compared to 3.1%oya in March. In sequential terms, we estimate that retail sales fell 0.5% m/ m sa in April, adding to the decline of 1.5% m/m sa in March. In further detail, auto sales (in nominal terms) fell 5.6%oya in April (vs. -3.7%oya in March), while retail sales ex-auto rose 3.2%oya (vs. 3.9%oya in March). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that auto sales fell 2.0% m/m sa in April, while retail sales excluding auto declined 0.3%m/m sa.

## Fine-tuning GDP growth trajectory

Taking into account the April data disappointment and the latest policy developments, we fine-tune our GDP growth forecast. We modestly revise down our 2Q GDP growth forecast to 3.5% q/q saar (previous forecast: 4.0%). Our 3Q growth forecast remains unchanged (at 4.5% q/q saar), while we revise up our 4Q forecast to 4.5% q/q saar (previously: 4.1% q/q saar). For full-year 2024, our growth forecast remains unchanged, at 5.2%.

## CPI inflation ticked up

China<sup>’</sup>s April CPI ticked up modestly, to 0.3%oya (flat m/m sa, Figure 4). The overall pricing environment remains rather soft: Taking the first four months together, headline CPI inflation averaged 0.1%oya (or +1.5% 3m/3m saar by April). Food prices eased further in April, -0.2% m/m sa (or -2.7%oya), with stabilization in pork prices (0.0%m/m nsa) and declines in vegetable prices (-3.7% m/m nsa) and fruit prices (-2.0% m/m nsa). Vehicle fuel prices rose 2.9% m/m nsa in April. Core CPI remained relatively steady, rising 0.7%oya (vs. 0.6%oya in March), or +0.1% m/m sa.

![](images/a0702b8f437cb2be773b2906eb3620b79bf34d9183530af85115af2d1e95129d.jpg)

Regarding the major factors driving recent CPI trends, food prices remained soft (though pork prices began to stabilize) and energy prices turned up along with global commodity prices, while there was volatility in travel and transport costs as holiday seasons come and go. Aside from these factors, general pricing power seems to have remained soft, and domestic demand has yet to gain momentum on a sustained basis.

## PPI deflation lingers

PPI deflation has lingered, as headline PPI fell 2.5%oya in April (vs. -2.8%oya in March, Figure 5) and fell 0.4% m/m sa. Energy-related PPI components rose moderately along with global energy prices. Meanwhile, producer prices for industrial materials came in somewhat mixed, with a decline in PPI for ferrous metal processing (including steel) and an upturn in PPI for non-ferrous metals (including copper). Auto-related PPI continued to ease.

Figure 5: China PPI and export prices
![](images/75592d90043ed5dbd13a50607e56fc228d1c0f01c57daf8c39401a2f1fa36b88.jpg)

We expect deflation to end, but low inflation to stay in 2024, considering the recent trend in global commodity prices, as domestic pork prices gradually stabilize, while annual CPI inflation will be supported by a low-base effect in the coming months. Meanwhile, the demand-supply imbalance and excess capacity concerns remain significant. Our forecast for full-year 2024 CPI inflation stands at 0.5%. Together with the prolonged PPI deflation pressure, the GDP deflator will likely stay in negative territory at least through the next two quarters.

## Big disappointment in credit report

April<sup>’</sup>s credit report was a big disappointment. TSF contracted 199 billion yuan in April, the first negative monthly TSF flow since the central bank began to release TSF data in 2011. TSF growth registered a new record low at 8.3%oya in April (vs. 8.7% in March, Figure 6), while loan growth stayed unchanged, at 9.6%. The slowdown in April credit growth was broad-based, led by a contraction in household loans (amid lingering weakness in home sales and consumer spending). Corporate loans came in soft, suggesting cautious corporate sentiment on the investment outlook (other than sectors of policy support), while corporate bond issuance also moderated notably. On the fiscal side, net government bond issuance showed the first monthly contraction since late 2018.

Figure 6: China TSF growth
![](images/fdc2303287d5fc91bfafe784ba2c8e9ad68a8f6a48efefde173b6f22f44acd13.jpg)

In our view, policymakers may have become somewhat complacent on the growth outlook after the strong 1Q GDP report. The disappointing April credit report reinforces the impor-

tance of maintaining growth-supportive macro policy going ahead (to avoid a repeat of the sudden loss of growth momentum, as happened this time last year).

## Accelerated home price decline

The NBS 70-city home prices recorded the fastest decline in the current housing cycle in April, down 3.5%oya or 0.6%m m nsa for new home prices, and down 6.8%oya or 0.9%m/m nsa for secondary home prices (Figure 7). This follows the 2.7%oya or 0.3%m/m fall for new home prices and the 5.9%oya or 0.5%m/m fall for secondary home prices in March. Compared to the previous month, new home prices declined in 64 cities in April (vs. 57 cities in March) and increased in 6 cities (vs. 11 cities in March). Across city tiers, new home prices fell 0.6%m/m in Tier-1 cities (vs. -0.1% March), with continued outperformance of Shanghai (+0.3%) and a 0.7%, 1.3% and 1.0% fall in Beijing, Guangzhou and Shenzhen, respectively. New home prices also dropped 0.5%m/m in Tier-2 cities and 0.6% in Tier-3 cities. As for the 70-city secondary home price, it dropped 1.1%m/m nsa in Tier-1 cities (vs. -0.7% in March) and was down 0.9% in both Tier-2 and 3 cities (vs. -0.5% in March). As of April, the 70 major cities<sup>’</sup> new home prices declined 5.8% compared to the peak in August 2021, along with an 11.5% drop for secondary home prices.

Figure 7: China 70-city housing prices 2010 Dec=100
![](images/b8ec9e2d74a29ca4519f6148e9fb04762934d30cb259ee19022202d2a7912478.jpg)

## U.S. tariff spike on targeted Chinese goods

On May 14, the Biden Administration announced tariff increases on selected Chinese goods, e.g., electric vehicles (from 25% to 100%), semiconductors (from 25% to 50%), lithium-ion batteries (from 7.5% to 25%), solar cells (from 25% to 50%), ship-to-shore cranes (from 0 to 25%) and some medical equipment (from 0-7.5% to 25-50%).

The immediate impact appears insignificant to us. First, the amount of affected Chinese goods totaled US\$18bn, only 4% of Chinese exports to the U.S. in 2023 (US\$506bn) or 0.5% of China<sup>’</sup>s total exports (US\$3.38 trillion). Second, some tariff increases will be effective in 2026 (e.g., lithium-ion batteries for non-auto use or medical-use gloves), providing some

Review of past week’s data

Week of May 20-24

time buffer for adjustment (or front-loaded exports before the new tariff rates become effective). The impact of this tariff increase is more about limiting the ability of Chinese companies to increase exports of these products in the U.S. market, in our view, which is different from the tariff increase under the Trump Administration (which was broad-based and started from Chinese goods with high market share in the U.S.).

However, the announcement raised an alarming bell on the uncertainty in trade relations that has been sidelined in recent years. This is the first tariff spike under the Biden Administration. The U.S. government<sup>’</sup>s attitude seems to be changing: Now tariffs are back on the table, and the discussion has shifted from structural adjustment in tariff rates (some increase, some reduction) to one-sided tariff increase on selected Chinese goods.

We see multiple uncertainties arising from the tariff actions by the U.S. government. First, how China will respond to this round of tariff increase, and whether the Biden Administration will expand the tariff increases to other Chinese products. Second, whether other countries will follow suit, e.g., Europe is considering a tariff increase on China<sup>’</sup>s electric vehicles, a much more important market for Chinese automakers. Third, whether the U.S. tariff increase will spill over to a third coun try to deal with trans-shipment or China<sup>’</sup>s overseas investment (link). For instance, the U.S. government has recently launched anti-dumping and anti-subsidy investigations into lithium-ion battery imports from Cambodia, Malaysia, Thailand and Vietnam (target destinations for China to re-direct exports or supply chains to bypass high U.S. tariffs). Finally, the biggest uncertainty is related to the U.S. Presidential election outcome, as Mr. Trump has threatened to hike tariffs on Chinese goods to 60% (and 10% for imports from other countries).

## Korea

• Employment growth continued in April

• 10-day exports fell in May

• Import prices turned up strongly with oil and FX effects

For the monetary policy meeting next week, the economic data suggest that a policy rate cut will not be needed in the very near term. Following the upside surprise in 1Q GDP growth, April employment continued a gradually rising trend. The April trade price data confirmed upward impacts from recent currency depreciation and global commodity prices, which are likely to hold headline CPI inflation up through mid-2024. We expect that the Bank of Korea will raise its annual GDP growth forecast materially from 2.1%y/y to near 2.5%, yet a continued upside risk bias will remain according to our outlook (JPMe 2.8%y/y). Given the above-potential GDP growth and above-target CPI inflation, the BoK is unlikely to pivot to a more dovish signal at the May meeting.

## Employment growth continued in April

Seasonally adjusted employment rose by 0.3%m/m, sa, in April, partly reversing March<sup>’</sup>s dip, marking the third monthly gain in the past four months. The active labor force rose by a similar extent, so the jobless rate stood flat at 2.8%. Sequential job growth stood at 1.6% 3m/3m, saar, in April for the second month, stabilizing around the 1% pace over the past half year (Figure 1). Zooming out, employment has been on a steady rise since it surpassed pre-pandemic levels in 2022.

Figure 1: Employment
![](images/514ca55b5d2e25b5699f72053755d67674f7524a0694f45d56e9b26986af5353.jpg)
Source: NSO, and J.P. Morgan

In April, job gains were largest in the agriculture sector recovering from falls in the past five months, followed by lei sure and accommodation and restaurant services, likely boosted by the recent sharp increase in tourist inflows. Manufacturing employment rose 0.8%m/m, sa, in April following the same amount of increase in March. Its sequential growth turned to a positive 2.2%3m/3m, saar, in April, after staying in the range of modestly negative 0.5-1.5% 3m/3m, saar, over the past half year. Job growth in manufacturing recently

caught up, though was still modest compared to production growth<sup>’</sup>s peak marked in 2H23 and followed by a moderation in 1Q24.

## Exports fell in early May

Seasonally adjusted, the first 10-day exports fell 2.7%m/m, sa, in May, after recovering by 4.2% in the full month of April from declines in the prior two months.This implies that sequential export growth may moderate to a slightly positive 0.7%3m/3m, saar, in April, after decelerating from a 24.1% peak in December, and dipping to marginally negative for one month in March. This generally aligns with our view that exports will broadly maintain modest positive growth despite corrections from 4Q23<sup>’</sup>s surge (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Korea customs exports
![](images/eaee2b37bd43da11e4b59ee1becd119f6dc9a87b4870c77921e579295d05977a.jpg)

Meanwhile, imports should fall 6.3%m/m, sa, in May. The strong monthly rise of imports in April followed by a fall in May likely reflects the recent moves in global crude oil prices. The country<sup>’</sup>s benchmark Dubai crude price has declined by 7% to date since it peaked in early April. The customs trade balance may mark a surplus of US\$5.3bn, sa, in May, according to the 10-day data, a recovery from a modest dip from US\$4.4bn-4.5bn in January-February to US\$3.3bn-3.4bn in March-April.

## Import prices turned up strongly

Korea<sup>’</sup>s import prices in local currency terms rose 3.4%m/m, sa, in April after falling 1.0% and 1.6% in the prior two months. They likely started to reflect upward pressures accumulated from the earlier increases in global crude oil prices and depreciation of the local currency. In April, import prices for energy rose strongly by 6.7%, as the country<sup>’</sup>s benchmark Dubai crude price rose 6.9%m/m, sa, during the month. Trade-weighted KRW depreciated 1.6% in April, resulting in an additional 2.6%pt monthly increase in KRW-based import prices compared to the ex-currency-effect prices. The increased external price pressures should delay stabilization of final headline CPI in the very near term. However, it may be temporary, as some abatement has followed this month. Global oil prices have recently declined from a peak in early-

April (Dubai -5.3%m/m, sa, in May mtd), and KRW NEER has appreciated modestly by 0.3% in May mtd, partially reversing April<sup>’</sup>s depreciation.

## ASEAN

• Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP as expected, capex slowdown could continue

• A bifurcated consumer suggests limited pricing power, core CPI stays benign and should remain so

Bank Indonesia’s focus shifted away from domestic considerations to external stability

• Risk of further hikes should US\$ strength persist

We expect sequential growth in Indonesia will continue to soften over the course of the year, reflecting the combination of slowing net exports, slowing domestic capex and the tightening in domestic financial and monetary conditions amid external uncertainty. These conditions would also suggest limited pricing power for those supplying domestic consumers, hence our forecast for benign CPI. We expect headline CPI to average around 2.5-3.0%oya this year, around the midpoint of the 2024 inflation target range of 1.5-3.5%oya. This forecast pencils in direct and indirect fiscal support to ensure price stability that comes on top of the already impressive set of supply-side measures implemented over the past several years.

Despite the domestic macro conditions, Bank Indonesia is now clearly focused on external conditions. Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s net FX reserves buffer, assessed via the IMF FX Reserves Adequacy metric, is relatively low compared to the region. Hence BI<sup>’</sup>s policy degrees of freedom are constrained if external conditions remain challenging. This, in our view, explains the unexpected hike in April. Rates are one of several tools, also including the SRBI, to maintain an FX reserves buffer. There have been periods of non-linearity in FX movements when net FX reserves experienced a material decline, perhaps explaining why preserving an FX reserves buffer is a priority amid a “high-for-long<sup>”</sup> environment in the DM.

## 1Q24 GDP, looking beneath the surface

Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s 1Q24 GDP came in as expected, up 5.1% oya (Consensus: 5.1%oya and J.P. Morgan: 4.4% oya) and rose 5.1% q/q, saar.

Since 2020, net exports have been a solid contributor to growth. But growth in them has been slowing since late 2023 with a slowdown in commodity-related exports. We expect that capex will also slow with a lag, as export-related incomes feed-through in capex, compounded also by a tightening in both monetary and external conditions (Figures 1 and 2).

Figure 1: Indonesia GDP Index, 2019=100, sa, real terms
![](images/fc2fe048211f8189bf9727efce6889b678626d8e74a9721a3f777ad0fa180741.jpg)

Figure 2: Indonesia GDP Index, 2019=100, sa, real terms
![](images/f468d85b400568486d4cf422f068db840aca252a62892946e6714b318963355b.jpg)

For private consumption, retail sales volume data depict a multi-speed expansion. Food-related spending has firmed even as other forms of discretionary spending have slowed. Four-wheeler sales slowed even as 2-wheeler sales recovered following some earlier softness (Figures 3 and 4). We are mindful that election-related spending could be providing a near-term boost to consumption and thus some slowing could be evident this quarter.

Figure 3: Indonesia retail sales index
![](images/8c4171266da98a30b07553693fb373038d13a254e3d1ebc72e34dd65369c0f61.jpg)
Aside from PCE, capital spending indicators look to be slowing into 2Q24, with both capital goods imports and heavy vehicle sales slowing in late 1Q24 and likely to continue slowing into 2Q24 amid an uncertain external environment and FX volatility, which tends to feed into corporate confidence.

Figure 4: Indonesia motor vehicle sales "000s, 2mma, sa, both scales
![](images/d1ea8c8848d4e0c1145ba9b8d1673602ff22d9b6c274b8c545d0ceb1da5150a2.jpg)

Within fixed asset investment, equipment spending has slowed and this has been offset by construction spending (Figure 5). The high-frequency capital spending proxies we track suggest further slowing towards the end of 1Q24 which could continue through this quarter and informs our forecast for a sequential slowing in growth (Figure 6).

Figure 5: Indonesia fixed investment
![](images/b109706b34386e7d391d93db10afc33ed1a42245f8c85177a6241b643d8ec415.jpg)

Figure 6: Indonesia capital goods imports and heavy vehicle sales Index, 2015=100, 2mma, sa, US\$ terms Index, 2015=100, 2mma, sa
![](images/3e63c58d17712fa4ff541a41e3086ecef31da39f1b4bd461779e58e5fd795af1.jpg)

## Underlying inflation benign

Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s April headline CPI came in a touch below expectations, up 3.0%oya (Consensus 3.1%oya and J.P. Morgan 2.7%oya) and up 0.1%m/m, sa. Core inflation came in at 1.8%oya – in-line with expectations - and was up 0.3%m/m, sa. Despite the Lebaran holidays, which tend to lead to seasonal increases in food prices, prices were subdued last month which speaks to the effectiveness of measures to mitigate price pressures. Outside of food, other prices remain benign

(Figure 7).
![](images/de9a9e129fd89c4743ffe7c54f96e7328807d81e9b70187d2c2305d2376c693a.jpg)

## BI shifts focus

Against our and consensus expectations, Bank Indonesia (BI) on April 24 raised its policy rate 25bp to 6.25%. This appears to have been in response to the rise in US rates, a related rise in risk premia, and rising geopolitical tensions which threaten higher energy prices.The unexpected rate hike brings into focus the importance of FX stability in the central bank<sup>’</sup>s reaction function, which has been sensitive to changes in net FX reserves, a proxy for BOP flows. Indonesia<sup>’</sup>s net FX reserves buffer relative to the IMF FX Reserves Adequacy metric is relatively low compared to the region, thus its policy degrees of freedom are constrained should external conditions remain challenging. In the past Indonesia has been exposed to periodic FX volatility (Figure 8).

Figure 8: Indonesia net FX reserves and FX rate
![](images/3c4e7c98af7012889c074fe0f1736f20b4f0990bb6fb37c984f0976a577196e6.jpg)

Given the focus on external conditions and the prospective path of a stronger US\$, the risk is that BI could hike again before the Fed begins to ease later this year, though we allocate a low probability to this risk. This is despite our view that CPI should remain at the lower bound of the CPI target range.

## India

● Headline CPI inflation remained benign, core-core remained unchanged

• WPI inflation edged higher in April driven by a pickup in commodity prices

• Trade deficit expectedly widened in April on seasonality

• Current account to swing back into a deficit in 2Q24

Headline CPI inflation in April inched lower as core prices remained soft, while food prices ex vegetables remained largely unchanged. The pick up in overall food prices, led by vegetables, was also unconcerning. In the coming months, headline inflation is expected to ease but these projections assume a normal monsoon. The outcome of monsoon would only be known in July/August. Consequently, we expect RBI to remain on hold in June.

April WPI inflation rose owing to firming commodity prices despite core prices falling for the fifth consecutive month. Separately, the merchandise trade deficit expectedly widened in April on seasonality. The underlying trade deficit remains contained much below 4Q23 average. The details were disappointing with exports showing a marked slowdown. Current account balance is expected to swing from a surplus in 1Q to a deficit of \~1.5% of GDP in 2Q.

## CPI remains benign

CPI dynamics remained benign in April with headline printing close to expectations at 4.8% oya (J.P. Morgan 4.9%; Consensus: 4.8%), easing from 4.9% in March (Figure 1). In sequential terms, consumer prices rose just 0.2% m/m, sa in April (0.1% increase in March).

Figure 1: Headline CPI inflation
![](images/474c6fc382a3ba1c84cfe9cb6253618e1ffb3d665728fa03657374c53e549cfd.jpg)
Core price momentum remains soft, with core-core increasing by 0.30% m/m, sa in April (0.26% in March). Furthermore, part of the increase in core is due to a sharp jump in gold prices. Core-core ex gold prices were up just 0.14% m/m, sa in April and 0.18% in March. On a year-on-year basis, core-core

inflation remained unchanged at a series-low of 3.4% (Figure 2). This softness reflects existing slack in the economy (output is still below the pre-pandemic path), lower input prices and easing inflation expectations.

Figure 2: Core-Core Inflation
![](images/38e99d18d11131af7ec410f0f0ef8075803274f92b77798d4e3a388530a4d019.jpg)

RBI<sup>’</sup>s focus in recent months has been on food prices and particularly the impact of the current heatwave. Food prices do not seem to have been materially impacted so far. Food ex vegetable prices were unchanged for the second month in a row (0% m/m, sa) helped by softer cereals prices. Vegetable prices have continued to rise (+1.9% m/m, sa in April) after the winter price correction was shallower than expected. All in all, food prices overall rose by a unconcerning 0.3% m/m, sa for the second month in row. On a year-on-year basis this translated into 7.9% oya in April (7.7% in March) (Figure 3).

Barring a shock in vegetable prices, inflation is expected to fall to \~4.5% in May, \~4% in June and then fall sharply to the 2-3% range in July and August on favorable base effects, before recovering to 4.5-5% in September. Key to all this, however, is a normal monsoon, which will not be known till July/August. Therefore, we expect RBI to remain on hold in the June review. But the benign inflation trajectory increases our conviction that monetary easing will occur later this year, barring fresh domestic or global shocks.

Figure 3: Food inflation
![](images/388e55132d814e2530e48c372431acb244e52af636c61b34827871692073f380.jpg)
Source: MoSPI, J.P. Morgan

## WPI inflation inches higher

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed 1.3% oya in April, picking up from 0.5% oya in March. Sequentially, it gained 0.2% on the back of three consecutive contractions owing to firming in commodity prices. However, core prices again fell 0.1% m/m, sa — the fifth consecutive month of contraction. While WPI inflation doesn<sup>’</sup>t have much bearing on RBI<sup>’</sup>s monetary policy, if the firming of WPI were to continue in the coming months, it could result in normalization of GDP deflator. Recall, that GDP deflator has remained very low the last few quarters reflecting weak WPI inflation.

Figure 4: Wholesale price index (WPI)
![](images/c714ed3dc13acc3a69f794242084355a54cb0ab3af136c0890907211d86a35b1.jpg)

## Trade deficit widens in April

The merchandise trade deficit widened to \$19bn in April from \$15.6bn (nsa) in March, which was unsurprising given the typical lower deficits in 1Q and March. While the underlying deficit (Trade deficit ex oil and gold) widened in April (\$6bn, nsa), it is contained and below 4Q23 average level of \$9.7 bn, nsa (Figure 5). However, the details of the report were disappointing with exports ex oil falling sharply (-8% m/m, sa) for a second month in row. This appears to be volume driven with manufacturing exports (a proxy for volume growth) showing similar trends. While non-oil-non-gold imports also sequentially contracted for a second month in a row, weakness there doesn<sup>’</sup>t appear to be volume driven as manufacturing imports sequentially rose in April. More broadly, imports of late have been volatile with no clear trend emerging from the data.

Figure 5: Trade deficit ex oil and gold
![](images/84610a33129ed856407b80856b987a44e384cbeaa37251119c2137e98643453e.jpg)

## Net services exports soften

Net services exports eased to \$12.6 bn in April from \$13.4 bn in March as services exports declined even as imports saw a slight increase. The softness in services exports is reflected in weak earnings reports by the information technology (IT) companies. Recall, IT services account for a sizeable part of IT service exports. The increase in the trade deficit coupled with the easing of net services exports has resulted in a pick up in the combined (merchandise and services) deficit from the lows seen in 4Q23 (Figure 6). All in all, underpinned by seasonality and slowing of exports the current account balance (CAB) is expected to swing sharply from a minor surplus in 1Q24 to a deficit of \~1.5% of GDP in 2Q24.

Figure 6: Combined (merchandise and services) trade deficit \$ bn, 3mma
![](images/f2863061759c99dedda1092edbf19912a85ecb6ce075bcd2f762e9d656f0c988.jpg)

## Asia Focus: Orders and inventories in the PMI

EM Asia ex-China and India<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing sector has posted a solid recovery since mid-2023, and the completion of the tech de-stocking cycle was one of the main drivers. The orders-to-inventories ratio in the regional PMI showed a turnaround in 4Q22, signaling that the inventory overhang has passed its worst, followed by actual production starting to reduce its sequential contraction in 1Q23 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Manufacturing PMI and output - EM Asia ex CN IN %3m/3m, saar
![](images/ad9e17cc39e7fc06714e8917c56510ad0e6cceb23b9410990f3a5e034f6851ec.jpg)
Source: Markit, J.P. Morgan

More recently, manufacturing production growth decelerated in 1Q24 after 2H23<sup>’</sup>s robust rebound. But PMI-implied inventory conditions suggest that the sector<sup>’</sup>s upturn may still have legs. The inventories PMI bottomed out in 4Q23, implying the finalization of the inventory drawdown, and combined with the orders PMI, the pair has stayed in the expansion phase—where both orders and inventories are on the rise—so far this year (Figures 2 and 3). That said, the current expansion could be more modest than in 1H21, when orders were especially strong on the pandemic-related surge in goods demand.

Figure 2: Stylized dynamics of orders and inventories New orders
![](images/37a72ecf25f7bee8d7028d13411cc18cdb68c5726722d69744b47a6898cc81c3.jpg)

Figure 3: Orders and inventories in mfg PMI - EM Asia ex CN IN New orders, DI
![](images/a96414e25be1567c77a09e276cc42953c6c705ef314cc719222e2f7b35b23a89.jpg)

At a country level, involuntary stock-building amid the demand slowdown was most distinguishable in Korea in 2H22-1H23, when the index pair stayed in the lower right quadrant (Figure 4), likely reflecting Korea<sup>’</sup>s specialization in highly cyclical memory chips. Vietnam experienced a delayed surge in the orders-to-inventories ratio compared to North Asia, possibly due to its downstream position in the regional tech supply chain (Figure 5). In Thailand, the ratio rather declined in 2023, likely reflecting Thailand<sup>’</sup>s lower exposure to the tech sector (putting greater weight to automobiles).

Figure 4: Orders and inventories in mfg PMI - Korea New orders, DI
![](images/9306393d323c337c342df463c3ad43dcbfbbb236f99f89ef9d3c66e8c04d9a0b.jpg)

Figure 5: Orders-to-inventories ratio in mfg PMI Ratio of DI, 3mma
![](images/7c6507459f7ebbdcba85c88caaaac1df765e509faf49b836cd2118e018504434.jpg)
Source: Markit, J.P. Morgan
